On the point-wise maximum and minimum $\max_i f_i({\boldsymbol x})$, $\min_i f_i({\boldsymbol x})$ of $m$ functions $f_1, \dots, f_m : D \to \R$, which statement is correct?
  1. the maximum of $m$ convex functions is concave
  2. the maximum of $m$ convex functions is convex only if $m = 2$
  3. the maximum of $m$ convex functions is convex, and the minimum of $m$ concave functions is concave
  4. the maximum of $m$ concave functions is concave, and the minimum of $m$ convex functions is convex
Explanation. The point-wise maximum of convex functions is convex and, passing to $-f_i$, the minimum of concave functions is concave (Observation 3.13). The minimum of $x^2$ and $(x-2)^2$ instead is not convex: with $p = 0$, $w = 2$, $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ we get $1 > 0$.
co-f-067 · level ipotesi · notes
Which results are needed to prove that ${\boldsymbol x} \mapsto \|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2$ is convex in $\R^n$?
  1. the inequality $2ab \le a^2 + b^2$, applied component by component
  2. triangle inequality and absolute homogeneity, with $\lambda, 1 - \lambda \ge 0$, and the convexity of $\R^n$
  3. the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, applied to ${\boldsymbol p}$ and ${\boldsymbol w}$, and the convexity of $\R^n$
  4. the positive semidefiniteness of the Hessian of $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2$ at every point
Explanation. We write $\|\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\|_2 \le |\lambda|\,\|{\boldsymbol p}\|_2 + |1-\lambda|\,\|{\boldsymbol w}\|_2$ and use $\lambda, 1-\lambda \ge 0$; the domain $\R^n$ is convex. Cauchy–Schwarz and $2ab \le a^2 + b^2$ are used instead for the squared norm; the norm is not differentiable at ${\boldsymbol 0}$.
co-f-068 · level ipotesi · notes
What is needed to prove $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^2 \le \lambda a^2 + (1-\lambda) b^2$ for every $a, b \in \R$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$?
  1. the expansion: the difference between the two sides equals $\lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2 \ge 0$
  2. the triangle inequality $|a + b| \le |a| + |b|$ with $\lambda \in [0,1]$
  3. the hypothesis $a, b \ge 0$, without which the inequality is false
  4. the case $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$, which extends to every $\lambda$ by symmetry
Explanation. Expanding, the right-hand side minus the left-hand side equals $\lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2$, a product of nonnegative factors. The sign of $a$ and $b$ matters for cubes, not for squares; the case $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ alone is not enough.
co-f-069 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $f, g : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ and $\alpha, \beta \in \R$. Which hypotheses does the proof that the combination $\alpha f + \beta g$ is convex in $D$ use?
  1. $f$ and $g$ convex in $D$ (hence $D$ convex), with $\alpha + \beta = 1$
  2. $f$ and $g$ twice differentiable, with positive semidefinite Hessian
  3. $f$ and $g$ convex in $D$ (hence $D$ convex), with arbitrary $\alpha, \beta \in \R$
  4. $f$ and $g$ convex in $D$ (hence $D$ convex), with $\alpha \ge 0$ and $\beta \ge 0$
Explanation. The two convexity inequalities are multiplied by $\alpha \ge 0$ and $\beta \ge 0$ (the direction does not change) and added. The constraint $\alpha + \beta = 1$ is not needed.
co-f-070 · level teorema · notes
On the squared Euclidean norm $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2^2 = x_1^2 + \dots + x_n^2$, seen as a function from $\R^n$ to $\R$, which statement is correct?
  1. it is convex only in $\{ {\boldsymbol x} : {\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0} \}$
  2. it is not convex, because the square of a convex function never is
  3. it is convex in the whole $\R^n$
  4. it is concave in the whole $\R^n$
Explanation. For every ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w}$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$ we have $\|\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\|_2^2 \le \lambda \|{\boldsymbol p}\|_2^2 + (1-\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol w}\|_2^2$ (Observation 3.9): it is enough to apply the inequality of squares to every component. The sign of the components does not matter.
co-f-071 · level facile · notes
For the convex function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}\|_2 + 1$ in $\R^2$, with ${\boldsymbol p} = \begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol w} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$, what are the two sides $f(\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w})$ and $\lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ of the convexity inequality?
  1. $\frac{7}{2}$ and $\frac{7}{2}$
  2. $\frac{7}{2}$ and $\frac{9}{2}$
  3. $\frac{9}{2}$ and $\frac{7}{2}$
  4. $\frac{5}{2}$ and $\frac{9}{2}$
Explanation. The midpoint is $(\frac{7}{2}, 4)$, at distance $\sqrt{(\frac{3}{2})^2 + 2^2} = \frac{5}{2}$ from $(2,2)$: $f = \frac{7}{2}$. Then $f({\boldsymbol p}) = 3 + 1 = 4$ and $f({\boldsymbol w}) = 4 + 1 = 5$, with average $\frac{9}{2}$. $\frac{5}{2}$ forgets the constant $1$.
co-f-072 · level facile · notes
The function $f : \R \to \R$, $f(x) = \max\{\, \frac{1}{10}(x-10)^2,\; \frac{1}{10}x^2 \,\}$, is:
  1. convex, as the maximum of two convex functions
  2. concave, because the graph has a corner at $x = 5$
  3. neither convex nor concave, because it is not differentiable at $x = 5$
  4. convex only in the interval $[0, 10]$
Explanation. The two parabolas are convex and the point-wise maximum of convex functions is convex (Observation 3.13). The corner at $x = 5$, where the parabolas meet, points downward and does not contradict convexity: differentiability is not needed.
co-f-073 · level facile · notes
When is a function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ concave in its domain $D$?
  1. if $D$ is convex and $f\big(\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda) {\boldsymbol w}\big) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for every ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and every $\lambda \in [0,1]$
  2. if $D$ is convex and $f\big(\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda) {\boldsymbol w}\big) \ge \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for at least one pair ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and one $\lambda \in [0,1]$
  3. if $f\big(\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda) {\boldsymbol w}\big) \ge \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for every ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and every $\lambda \in [0,1]$, whatever $D$ is
  4. if $D$ is convex and $f\big(\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda) {\boldsymbol w}\big) \ge \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for every ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and every $\lambda \in [0,1]$
Explanation. Definition 3.2: the value at the convex combination is at least the convex combination of the values, for all pairs and all $\lambda$, over a convex domain. With $\le$ we get the definition of convex function.
co-f-074 · level facile · notes
Let $f : [a,b] \to \R$ be a concave function and let $p, w \in [a,b]$. Where is the graph of $f$ between $p$ and $w$ with respect to the segment (chord) joining the points $(p, f(p))$ and $(w, f(w))$?
  1. above the chord only if $f(p) = f(w)$
  2. below the chord only if $f$ is differentiable
  3. above the chord or on it
  4. below the chord or on it
Explanation. The points of the chord have height $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w)$ and concavity gives $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) \ge \lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w)$: the graph lies above (or on) the chord. «Below» describes convex functions.
co-f-154 · level facile · notes
A function $f: D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ is convex if $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$ ($D$ convex). Which function of one variable is convex on $\R$?
  1. $f(x) = x^3$
  2. $f(x) = \sin x$
  3. $f(x) = |x|$
  4. $f(x) = -x^2$
Explanation. The absolute value is a norm: it is convex.
co-f-155 · level facile · notes
A function $f: D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ is convex if $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$ ($D$ convex). For $f(x) = x^2$, ${\boldsymbol p} = 0$, ${\boldsymbol w} = 2$, $\lambda = \frac12$: what are the two sides of the inequality?
  1. $4 \le 2$
  2. $1 \le 2$
  3. $2 \le 1$
  4. $1 \le 1$
Explanation. $f(1) = 1$ and $\frac12 f(0) + \frac12 f(2) = 2$.
co-f-156 · level facile · notes
When is $f: D \to \R$ ($D$ convex) concave?
  1. $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) \ge \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w}$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$
  2. $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w}$ and $\lambda$
  3. $f({\boldsymbol p}) \le f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w}$
  4. $f$ is decreasing in every variable
Explanation. The chord lies below the graph.
co-f-157 · level facile · notes
Which function is concave on $(0, +\infty)$?
  1. $f(x) = x^2$
  2. $f(x) = \frac1x$
  3. $f(x) = e^x$
  4. $f(x) = \sqrt{x}$
Explanation. The square root has chords below the graph.
co-f-158 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about a linear function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ is correct?
  1. It is neither convex nor concave
  2. It is concave only if ${\boldsymbol c} \le {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. It is both convex and concave
  4. It is convex but not concave
Explanation. The defining inequality holds with equality.
co-f-159 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that $f$ is concave it suffices to show that $-f$ is convex. Which fact justifies it?
  1. That the domain is bounded
  2. The sign $-$ reverses the inequality
  3. That $f$ is positive
  4. That $f$ is quadratic
Explanation. It is the observation linking convex and concave functions.
co-f-160 · level facile · notes
The Euclidean norm $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2$ on $\R^n$ is:
  1. convex
  2. concave
  3. neither convex nor concave
  4. convex only for $n = 1$
Explanation. Triangle inequality and homogeneity give convexity.
co-f-161 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about norms on $\R^n$ is correct?
  1. Only the Euclidean norm is convex
  2. Norms are concave
  3. The $\ell_\infty$ norm is not convex
  4. Every norm is a convex function
Explanation. $\|\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\| \le \lambda\|{\boldsymbol p}\| + (1-\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol w}\|$.
co-f-162 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof that a norm is convex, which norm properties are used?
  1. Differentiability
  2. That the domain is bounded
  3. Triangle inequality and homogeneity
  4. Only positivity
Explanation. $\|\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\| \le \|\lambda{\boldsymbol p}\| + \|(1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\|$.
co-f-163 · level facile · notes
For a norm $\|\cdot\|$ and a radius $r > 0$, the set $\{{\boldsymbol x} : \|{\boldsymbol x}\| \le r\}$ is:
  1. unbounded
  2. convex
  3. not convex in general
  4. finite
Explanation. Sublevel set of a convex function.
co-f-164 · level facile · notes
The function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2^2 = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol x}$ is:
  1. convex
  2. concave
  3. linear
  4. neither convex nor concave
Explanation. It is a quadratic with ${\boldsymbol Q} = {\boldsymbol I}$, positive semidefinite.
co-f-165 · level facile · notes
If $f$ and $g$ are convex on $D$, which function is certainly convex?
  1. $f - g$
  2. $f \cdot g$
  3. $f / g$
  4. $f + g$
Explanation. A sum of convex functions is convex; differences and products are not.
co-f-166 · level facile · notes
If $f_1, f_2$ are convex, the function $g({\boldsymbol x}) = \max\{f_1({\boldsymbol x}), f_2({\boldsymbol x})\}$ is:
  1. linear
  2. neither convex nor concave in general
  3. convex
  4. concave
Explanation. The pointwise maximum of convex functions is convex.
co-f-293 · level facile · notes
A function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$, with $D$ convex, is convex if $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$. Is the function $f(x) = x^2$ with domain $D = [-2, -1] \cup [1, 2]$ convex on its domain according to this definition?
  1. yes, because $x^2$ is a convex function
  2. yes: the inequality holds on the two intervals
  3. no: $x^2$ is not convex on $[1, 2]$
  4. no: the definition requires a convex domain
Explanation. $D$ is not convex (it contains $-1$ and $1$ but not $0$), so the definition does not apply: for example $f(\frac{1}{2}(-1) + \frac{1}{2} \cdot 1)$ is not even defined.
co-f-294 · level facile · notes
A function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$, with $D$ convex, is convex if $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$. If $f$ is convex on $[0, 4]$ with $f(0) = 1$ and $f(4) = 9$, which bound holds for $f(1)$?
  1. $f(1) \le 5$
  2. $f(1) = 3$
  3. $f(1) \le 3$
  4. $f(1) \ge 3$
Explanation. $1 = \frac{3}{4} \cdot 0 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot 4$, hence $f(1) \le \frac{3}{4} \cdot 1 + \frac{1}{4} \cdot 9 = 3$: the graph lies below the chord. $5$ is the average with weights $\frac{1}{2}$, which are wrong.
co-f-295 · level facile · notes
A function $f$ is concave on $[0, 2]$ if $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) \ge \lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w)$ for all $p, w \in [0,2]$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$. If $f(0) = 0$ and $f(2) = 4$, which bound holds for $f(1)$?
  1. $f(1) = 2$
  2. $f(1) \ge 2$
  3. $f(1) \le 2$
  4. $f(1) \ge 4$
Explanation. With $p = 0$, $w = 2$, $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$: $f(1) \ge \frac{1}{2} \cdot 0 + \frac{1}{2} \cdot 4 = 2$. The graph of a concave function lies above the chord, not necessarily on it.
co-f-296 · level facile · notes
For all $a, b \in \R$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$ we have $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^2 \le \lambda a^2 + (1-\lambda) b^2$. What are the two sides for $a = 1$, $b = 5$, $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$?
  1. $9 \le 13$
  2. $13 \le 9$
  3. $9 \le 9$
  4. $6 \le 13$
Explanation. $\big(\frac{1}{2} + \frac{5}{2}\big)^2 = 3^2 = 9$ and $\frac{1}{2} \cdot 1 + \frac{1}{2} \cdot 25 = 13$; the difference is $\lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2 = \frac{1}{4} \cdot 16 = 4$.
co-f-297 · level facile · notes
Is the function $f(x) = x^2 + 10$ on $\R$ convex?
  1. no: the constant $10$ makes it nonconvex
  2. it is convex only for $x \ge 0$
  3. no: it is concave, because $f(x) \ge 10$ everywhere
  4. yes: the constant does not change the inequality
Explanation. $\lambda \cdot 10 + (1-\lambda) \cdot 10 = 10$: the constant appears equally on both sides and the inequality of $x^2$ remains valid.
co-f-298 · level facile · notes
The function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2$ on $\R^2$, whose graph is a paraboloid, is:
  1. neither convex nor concave
  2. convex only in the first quadrant
  3. convex, as a sum of two convex functions
  4. concave, because the graph is a curved surface
Explanation. $x_1^2$ and $x_2^2$ are convex (inequality of squares), and the sum of convex functions is convex; it is also $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2^2$.
co-f-299 · level facile · notes
A norm on $\R^2$ must be positive, absolutely homogeneous ($\|\alpha{\boldsymbol p}\| = |\alpha|\,\|{\boldsymbol p}\|$) and satisfy the triangle inequality. Which function is <em>not</em> a norm?
  1. $\sqrt{x_1^2 + x_2^2}$
  2. $x_1^2 + x_2^2$
  3. $|x_1| + |x_2|$
  4. $\max\{|x_1|, |x_2|\}$
Explanation. $x_1^2 + x_2^2$ is not homogeneous: doubling ${\boldsymbol x}$ quadruples the value. The others are the norms $1$, $\infty$ and $2$.
co-f-300 · level facile · notes
What is the convex function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}\|_2^2$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $5$
  2. $\sqrt{5}$
  3. $3$
  4. $25$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, and the squared Euclidean norm is $2^2 + 1^2 = 5$; $\sqrt{5}$ is the norm, $3$ the norm $1$.
co-f-301 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = -|x|$ on $\R$ is:
  1. convex, because $|x|$ is convex
  2. neither convex nor concave
  3. both convex and concave
  4. concave, because $|x|$ is convex
Explanation. $f$ is convex if and only if $-f$ is concave: $|x|$ is convex, hence $-|x|$ is concave. It is not convex as well (for example $f(0) = 0 > -1 = \frac{1}{2}f(-1) + \frac{1}{2}f(1)$).
co-f-302 · level facile · notes
Why is $|x| = \max\{x, -x\}$ a convex function on $\R$?
  1. because it is the sum of two linear functions
  2. because it is differentiable at every point
  3. because it is the maximum of two linear functions
  4. because it is the minimum of two linear functions
Explanation. The maximum of convex functions is convex, and linear functions are convex. The minimum of linear functions is concave; $|x|$ is not differentiable at $0$.
co-f-303 · level ipotesi · notes
The maximum of two convex functions is convex. Is the <em>minimum</em> of two convex functions convex?
  1. no: the minimum of convex functions is always concave
  2. not always: $\min\{(x-1)^2, (x+1)^2\}$ is not
  3. yes, always, like the maximum
  4. yes, if the two functions intersect
Explanation. $g(x) = \min\{(x-1)^2, (x+1)^2\}$ has $g(-1) = g(1) = 0$ and $g(0) = 1 > 0$: the graph lies above the chord. It is not concave either ($g(3) = 4 < \frac{1}{2}(g(1) + g(5)) = 8$).
co-f-304 · level ipotesi · notes
If $g : \R^n \to \R$ is convex, the set $\{ {\boldsymbol x} : g({\boldsymbol x}) \le 0 \}$ is convex. What about the set $\{ {\boldsymbol x} : g({\boldsymbol x}) \ge 0 \}$?
  1. not always: it may not be
  2. yes, always, like $\{ g \le 0 \}$
  3. yes, if $g$ is also continuous
  4. it is always the empty set
Explanation. The property holds for constraints $g \le 0$ with $g$ convex. $\{x_1^2 + x_2^2 \ge 1\}$ contains $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ but not the origin.
co-f-305 · level ipotesi · notes
The function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2^2$ is convex. Is it also a norm (positive, absolutely homogeneous, with the triangle inequality)?
  1. yes: it is the square of a norm
  2. yes: it is convex and nonnegative
  3. no: it is not a convex function
  4. no: it is not absolutely homogeneous
Explanation. A norm satisfies $\|\alpha{\boldsymbol x}\| = |\alpha|\,\|{\boldsymbol x}\|$; the square multiplies by $\alpha^2$. Being convex and nonnegative is not enough to be a norm.
co-f-306 · level ipotesi · notes
A function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$, with $D$ convex, is convex if $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in D$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$. A function $f$ on $[0, 2]$ satisfies the inequality for $p = 0$, $w = 2$ and every $\lambda \in [0,1]$. What can be concluded?
  1. that $f$ is convex, if it is continuous
  2. that $f$ is not concave on $[0, 2]$
  3. nothing: all pairs $p, w$ are needed
  4. that $f$ is convex on $[0, 2]$
Explanation. A single pair is not enough: $f(x) = -x^2(2-x)^2$ lies below the chord between $0$ and $2$ (which is zero), but with $p = 0$, $w = \frac{1}{2}$, $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ we have $f(\frac{1}{4}) = -\frac{49}{256} > -\frac{9}{32} = \frac{1}{2} f(0) + \frac{1}{2} f(\frac{1}{2})$. A linear function passes the check and is concave.
co-f-307 · level ipotesi · notes
The inequality $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^2 \le \lambda a^2 + (1-\lambda) b^2$ holds for $\lambda \in [0,1]$. Does it also hold for $\lambda = 2$, with $a = 1$ and $b = 0$?
  1. no: the left side equals $2$, the right side $4$
  2. no: the left side equals $4$, the right side $2$
  3. yes: it holds for every $\lambda \in \R$
  4. yes: with $b = 0$ the two sides coincide
Explanation. $\big(2 \cdot 1 + (-1) \cdot 0\big)^2 = 4$ and $2 \cdot 1 + (-1) \cdot 0 = 2$. The difference is $\lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2$, which for $\lambda = 2$ equals $-2 < 0$: we need $\lambda \in [0,1]$.
co-f-334 · level teorema · notes
Let $f, g : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ be convex in $D$. Which statement is correct?
  1. the product $f \cdot g$ is convex in $D$
  2. the difference $f - g$ is convex in $D$
  3. $\alpha f + \beta g$ is convex in $D$ for all $\alpha, \beta \ge 0$
  4. $\alpha f + \beta g$ is convex in $D$ for all $\alpha, \beta \in \R$
Explanation. Nonnegative coefficients preserve the direction of the convexity inequality. With a negative coefficient one can get a concave function ($x^2 - 2x^2 = -x^2$); the product may not be convex either ($x \cdot x^2 = x^3$).
co-f-335 · level teorema · notes
On a function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$, with $D$ convex, which statement is correct?
  1. $f$ is convex in $D$ if and only if $f$ is increasing in $D$
  2. $f$ is convex in $D$ if and only if $-f$ is concave in $D$
  3. $f$ is convex in $D$ if and only if $\frac{1}{f}$ is concave in $D$
  4. if $f$ is convex in $D$, then it is not concave in $D$
Explanation. Multiplying by $-1$ reverses the inequality. A linear function is both convex and concave, and $|x|$ is convex without being increasing; the reciprocal has no general link with concavity.
co-f-336 · level teorema · notes
For all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in \R^n$ and every $\lambda \in [0,1]$, which inequality on the Euclidean norm is correct?
  1. $\|\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\|_2 \le \lambda\|{\boldsymbol p}\|_2 + (1-\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol w}\|_2$
  2. $\|\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\|_2 \ge \lambda\|{\boldsymbol p}\|_2 + (1-\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol w}\|_2$
  3. $\|\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\|_2 = \lambda\|{\boldsymbol p}\|_2 + (1-\lambda)\|{\boldsymbol w}\|_2$
  4. $\|\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}\|_2 \le \|{\boldsymbol p}\|_2\, \|{\boldsymbol w}\|_2$
Explanation. It is the convexity of the Euclidean norm written as an inequality. Equality does not hold in general: with ${\boldsymbol w} = -{\boldsymbol p} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ the left side is $0$ and the right side is $\|{\boldsymbol p}\|_2$.
co-f-383 · level facile · notes
What is the convex function $f(x) = \max\{\, \frac{1}{10}(x-10)^2,\; \frac{1}{10}x^2 \,\}$ at the point $x = 5$?
  1. $10$
  2. $\frac{5}{2}$
  3. $5$
  4. $0$
Explanation. At $x = 5$ both parabolas equal $\frac{25}{10} = \frac{5}{2}$, hence so does their maximum; it is the point where $f$ is smallest.
co-f-384 · level facile · notes
The $q$-norm of ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n$ is $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_q = \big(\sum_j |x_j|^q\big)^{1/q}$, with $q \ge 1$. For which value of $q$ does it coincide with the Euclidean norm?
  1. $q = 2$
  2. $q = 1$
  3. $q = \infty$
  4. $q = \frac{1}{2}$
Explanation. With $q = 2$ we get $\sqrt{\sum_j x_j^2}$. With $q = 1$ we have the Manhattan norm, in the limit $q \to \infty$ the maximum norm; $q = \frac{1}{2} < 1$ does not give a norm.
co-f-385 · level facile · notes
What is the convex function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}\|_2 + 1$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $5$
  2. $26$
  3. $8$
  4. $6$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, with Euclidean norm $\sqrt{9 + 16} = 5$, plus $1$. With $26$ the squared norm is used, with $8$ the norm $1$.
co-f-386 · level facile · notes
Does the point $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ belong to the convex set $\{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : |x_1| + |x_2| - 2 \le 0,~ x_1 + x_2 - 1 \le 0 \}$?
  1. no: it violates the first constraint
  2. no: boundary points are excluded
  3. yes: $1 - 2 \le 0$ and $1 - 1 \le 0$
  4. no: it violates the second constraint
Explanation. $|1| + |0| - 2 = -1 \le 0$ and $1 + 0 - 1 = 0 \le 0$: the point satisfies both constraints (the second with equality, on the boundary, which is included).
co-f-387 · level facile · notes
For $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2x_1 - x_2 + 3$ on $\R^2$, when does $f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) = \lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ hold?
  1. never, because of the constant $3$
  2. for all ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w}$ and every $\lambda \in [0,1]$
  3. only for $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$, at the midpoint
  4. only if ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. A linear function plus a constant satisfies the definition with equality (the constant is written $3 = 3\lambda + 3(1-\lambda)$): it is both convex and concave.
co-f-388 · level facile · notes
What is the norm $\infty$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = \max_j |x_j|$, of the vector ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $3$
  2. $1$
  3. $5$
  4. $\sqrt{11}$
Explanation. The maximum of the absolute values $1, 1, 3$ is $3$. $5$ is the norm $1$, $\sqrt{11}$ the norm $2$; $1$ is the maximum without absolute values.

4. Quadratic functions (56)

co-f-075 · level facile · notes
The non-symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ -2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$ defines the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$. Which symmetric matrix defines the same function?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 4 \\ 4 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -2 \\ -2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. We use $\tfrac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol Q} + {\boldsymbol Q}')$: off the diagonal $\frac{4 + (-2)}{2} = 1$. Copying only one of the two entries changes the coefficient of $x_1 x_2$.
co-f-076 · level facile · notes
With ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol x} = (x_1, x_2)'$, how is the quadratic function ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ written?
  1. $2x_1^2 + 2x_1x_2 + 3x_2^2$
  2. $2x_1^2 + x_1x_2 + 3x_2^2$
  3. $2x_1^2 + 4x_1x_2 + 3x_2^2$
  4. $4x_1^2 + 2x_1x_2 + 6x_2^2$
Explanation. $\sum_i \sum_j q_{ij} x_i x_j = 2x_1^2 + 1\cdot x_1x_2 + 1\cdot x_2x_1 + 3x_2^2$: both off-diagonal entries contribute to $x_1 x_2$.
co-f-077 · level facile · notes
What is the gradient of the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$, with ${\boldsymbol Q}$ symmetric?
  1. ${\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$
  2. $2\,{\boldsymbol Q}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}$
  4. $2\,{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$
Explanation. Observation 4.2: $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_k} = \sum_j (q_{kj} + q_{jk}) x_j = 2\sum_j q_{kj} x_j$ because ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is symmetric. $2{\boldsymbol Q}$ is the Hessian.
co-f-078 · level facile · notes
What is the Hessian of $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$, with ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$ symmetric, ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$ and $d \in \R$?
  1. $2\,{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}$
  2. $2\,{\boldsymbol Q} + {\boldsymbol c}$
  3. $2\,{\boldsymbol Q}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol Q}$
Explanation. The linear part and the constant have zero second-order derivatives: $\nabla^2 g({\boldsymbol x}) = 2{\boldsymbol Q}$, constant. $2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}$ is the gradient.
co-f-079 · level facile · notes
With ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$, what is the gradient of the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = (4x_1 - 2x_2,\; -2x_1 + 4x_2)$, which at $(1,0)$ equals $(4,-2)$ (Example 4.5). $(2,-1) = {\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x}$ forgets the factor $2$.
co-f-080 · level facile · notes
When is a square symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$ positive semidefinite?
  1. when ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n$
  2. when all its entries are nonnegative
  3. when all its diagonal entries are nonnegative
  4. when ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for at least one ${\boldsymbol x} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. Definition 4.5. Nonnegative entries or diagonal are not enough: ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $-2$ with ${\boldsymbol x} = (1,-1)$.
co-f-081 · level facile · notes
Is the symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$ positive semidefinite, that is, does ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ hold for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$?
  1. yes: it is symmetric and its trace equals $0 \ge 0$
  2. yes: the first diagonal entry, $1$, is positive
  3. no: with ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ we get ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = 0$, but $> 0$ is required
  4. no: with ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ we get ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = -1 < 0$
Explanation. The function is $x_1^2 - x_2^2$ and equals $-1$ at $(0,1)$: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not positive semidefinite and the function is not convex. Symmetry, trace and one positive diagonal entry are not enough; the value $0$ is allowed by the definition.
co-f-082 · level facile · notes
For ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$ we have ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = x_1^2 + x_2^2 + (x_1 - x_2)^2$. What follows?
  1. the quadratic function is concave
  2. the quadratic function is convex only where $x_1 \ge x_2$
  3. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite and the quadratic function is convex
  4. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not positive semidefinite, because it has negative entries
Explanation. A sum of squares is $\ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite and, since a quadratic function is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite (Corollary 4.8), the function is convex. The negative off-diagonal entries do not matter.
co-f-083 · level facile · notes
With ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$ symmetric, ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$ and $d \in \R$, when is the function $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$ convex in $\R^n$?
  1. always, because the linear part is convex
  2. if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite
  3. if and only if ${\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. if and only if $d \ge 0$
Explanation. Adding a linear function and a constant does not change convexity: only ${\boldsymbol Q}$ decides, and ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x}$ is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite.
co-f-084 · level facile · notes
What is the shape of the level sets $\{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = k \}$, nonempty and with $k \ne 0$, of the quadratic functions $x_1^2 + x_2^2$ and $x_1^2 - x_2^2$?
  1. circles for the first, hyperbolas for the second
  2. hyperbolas for the first, circles for the second
  3. circles for both
  4. pairs of parallel lines for both
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol Q}$ equal to the identity we get circles (Example 4.2); with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \mathrm{diag}(1,-1)$ hyperbolas (Example 4.9). Pairs of parallel lines appear when some direction does not change the value of the function.
co-f-085 · level facile · notes
Which symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ gives $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 3x_1^2 - 4x_1x_2 + x_2^2$ in the form ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & -4 \\ -4 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & -4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & -2 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The coefficient of $x_1x_2$ is $q_{12} + q_{21} = 2q_{12} = -4$, hence $q_{12} = q_{21} = -2$. The matrix $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & -4 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives the same function, but it is not symmetric.
co-f-086 · level facile · notes
Is the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + 4x_1x_2 + x_2^2 = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$, with ${\boldsymbol Q}$ symmetric, convex in $\R^2$?
  1. yes: all the coefficients are positive, so the function is a sum of convex terms
  2. no: it is concave, because the mixed term $4x_1x_2$ dominates the two squares
  3. no: with ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ it equals $-2 < 0$, so ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not positive semidefinite
  4. yes: the coefficients of $x_1^2$ and $x_2^2$ are positive, so ${\boldsymbol Q}$ has a positive diagonal
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $1 - 4 + 1 = -2$: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not positive semidefinite, hence the function is not convex ($4x_1x_2$ is not a convex term). It is not concave either: at $(1,0)$ it equals $1 > 0$.
co-f-087 · level facile · notes
With ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$ symmetric and ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$, what is the gradient of $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x}$ at the point ${\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol 0}$?
  1. $2\,{\boldsymbol Q}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol c}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. $2\,{\boldsymbol c}$
Explanation. $\nabla g({\boldsymbol x}) = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}$, which at ${\boldsymbol 0}$ equals ${\boldsymbol c}$.
co-f-088 · level media · notes
For the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$, what are $f({\boldsymbol x})$ and the gradient $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x})$ at the point ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = 6$, $\nabla f = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = 6$, $\nabla f = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = 3$, $\nabla f = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = 10$, $\nabla f = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $2\cdot 1 - 2\cdot 1\cdot 2 + 2\cdot 4 = 6$; ${\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = (0, 3)$ and $\nabla f = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = (0, 6)$. Forgetting the mixed terms we would get $10$.
co-f-089 · level media · notes
Which of the following symmetric matrices ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, that is, has ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}' \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix} {\boldsymbol x} = (x_1 + x_2)^2 \ge 0$. The others give negative values: with $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and with $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ the vector $(1,-1)$ gives $-2$; with $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -2 \end{pmatrix}$ the vector $(0,1)$ gives $-2$.
co-f-090 · level media · notes
At which point ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$ does the gradient of the quadratic function $g({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2x_1 + 4x_2 + 1$ vanish?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla g = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c} = (2x_1 - 2,\; 2x_2 + 4) = {\boldsymbol 0}$ for $x_1 = 1$, $x_2 = -2$. Typical mistakes: sign of ${\boldsymbol c}$ ($(-1,2)$), factor $2$ forgotten ($(2,-4)$).
co-f-091 · level teorema · notes
Let $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ be the quadratic function of a symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. if $f$ is convex, then ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} > 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$
  2. $f$ is convex in $\R^n$ if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite
  3. $f$ is convex in $\R^n$ if and only if all the entries of ${\boldsymbol Q}$ are nonnegative
  4. $f$ is convex in $\R^n$ if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is diagonal
Explanation. It is Corollary 4.8: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite implies $f$ convex, and conversely. Requiring ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} > 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \ne {\boldsymbol 0}$ is too strong: $(x_1 + x_2)^2$ is convex but equals $0$ at $(1,-1)$.
co-f-092 · level teorema · notes
For the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$, with ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$ symmetric, which statement on gradient and Hessian is correct?
  1. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ and $\nabla^2 f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,{\boldsymbol Q}$ at every point
  2. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ and $\nabla^2 f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol Q}$ at every point
  3. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ and $\nabla^2 f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$
  4. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,{\boldsymbol Q}$ and $\nabla^2 f({\boldsymbol x})$ is the zero matrix
Explanation. Differentiating $\sum_i \sum_j q_{ij} x_i x_j$ gives $\nabla f = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x}$ and, differentiating again, $\nabla^2 f = 2{\boldsymbol Q}$: the Hessian of a quadratic function is constant. Without the factor $\frac{1}{2}$ in front of ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ the factor $2$ appears.
co-f-093 · level teorema · notes
On the quadratic functions ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ defined by a square matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$, possibly non-symmetric, which statement is correct?
  1. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ and ${\boldsymbol Q}'$ define different quadratic functions
  2. only symmetric matrices define quadratic functions
  3. $\tfrac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol Q} + {\boldsymbol Q}')$ defines the quadratic function of ${\boldsymbol Q}$ multiplied by $\frac{1}{2}$
  4. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ and $\tfrac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol Q} + {\boldsymbol Q}')$ define the same quadratic function
Explanation. For $i < j$ the terms $q_{ij} x_i x_j + q_{ji} x_j x_i$ depend only on the sum $q_{ij} + q_{ji}$: we can always assume ${\boldsymbol Q}$ symmetric without loss of generality.
co-f-094 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that, if the symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, then $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ is convex, what is used (with ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \in \R^n$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$)?
  1. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ diagonal with nonnegative entries, so that $f$ is a sum of squares
  2. the triangle inequality for the Euclidean norm, with $\lambda \ge 0$ and $1 - \lambda \ge 0$
  3. the identity $\lambda^2 = \lambda - \lambda(1-\lambda)$, ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for ${\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w}$, and $\lambda(1-\lambda) \ge 0$
  4. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive definite, the single case $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ and the symmetry of ${\boldsymbol Q}$
Explanation. We reach ${\lambda {\boldsymbol p}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol w}} - \lambda(1-\lambda)({\boldsymbol p}-{\boldsymbol w})'{\boldsymbol Q}({\boldsymbol p}-{\boldsymbol w})$ and the subtracted term is $\ge 0$.
co-f-095 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that, if $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ (with ${\boldsymbol Q}$ symmetric) is convex, then ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, what is used?
  1. the hypothesis that ${\boldsymbol Q}$ has positive diagonal entries, with $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$
  2. the convexity inequality with $\lambda \in (0,1)$, the division by $\lambda(1-\lambda) > 0$ and the choice ${\boldsymbol p} = {\boldsymbol x}$, ${\boldsymbol w} = {\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. the convexity inequality with $\lambda = 0$, without divisions, and the choice ${\boldsymbol p} = {\boldsymbol x}$, ${\boldsymbol w} = {\boldsymbol 0}$
  4. the hypothesis that ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, applied to the vector ${\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w}$
Explanation. Convexity gives $\lambda(1-\lambda)({\boldsymbol p}-{\boldsymbol w})'{\boldsymbol Q}({\boldsymbol p}-{\boldsymbol w}) \ge 0$; we divide by $\lambda(1-\lambda) > 0$ and choose ${\boldsymbol w} = {\boldsymbol 0}$. With $\lambda = 0$ the inequality is trivial; assuming ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite would assume the thesis.
co-f-096 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that, if $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$ is convex, then ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, what is needed?
  1. ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = g({\boldsymbol x}) + (-{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} - d)$ is a sum of convex functions, and ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ convex implies ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite
  2. the hypothesis ${\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ and $d = 0$, which reduces $g$ to ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ without loss of generality
  3. the fact that the Hessian of $g$ is zero, because $g$ is quadratic
  4. the hypothesis $g$ concave, applied to $-g$ to get the convexity of ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$
Explanation. $-{\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} - d$ is linear plus a constant, hence convex; ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ is the sum of two convex functions, and a convex quadratic function has ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite. We cannot assume ${\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol 0}$: it is exactly what must be handled.
co-f-097 · level facile · notes
For the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2$ (matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ equal to the identity), what is the Euclidean norm of the gradient $\|\nabla f({\boldsymbol x})\|_2$ at every point of the level set $\{ {\boldsymbol x} : f({\boldsymbol x}) = 1 \}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $\sqrt{2}$
  3. $4$
  4. $2$
Explanation. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = 2{\boldsymbol x}$ and the points of the level set have $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 1$: the norm of the gradient is $2$. $1$ forgets the factor $2$.
co-f-098 · level facile · notes
For the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$, that is, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2x_1^2 - 2x_1x_2 + 2x_2^2$, what is the shape of the level sets $\{ {\boldsymbol x} : f({\boldsymbol x}) = k \}$ with $k > 0$?
  1. hyperbolas centered at the origin
  2. pairs of parallel lines
  3. ellipses centered at the origin
  4. circles centered at the origin
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite ($f = x_1^2 + x_2^2 + (x_1 - x_2)^2$) and $f > 0$ away from the origin: the level sets are ellipses, stretched along the bisector $x_1 = x_2$ because of the mixed term. Circles would appear only without mixed term and with equal coefficients.
co-f-167 · level facile · notes
Which function is a quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x}$ with symmetric ${\boldsymbol Q}$?
  1. $x_1^2 + 1$
  2. $3x_1^2 - 4x_1x_2 + x_2^2$
  3. $3x_1^2 + x_2$
  4. $x_1 x_2 x_3$
Explanation. Only degree-two terms: ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & -2 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$.
co-f-168 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x}$ is correct?
  1. $f({\boldsymbol 0}) = 0$ and $f(t{\boldsymbol x}) = t^2 f({\boldsymbol x})$
  2. $f$ is linear
  3. $f({\boldsymbol x}) \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol Q}$
  4. $f(t{\boldsymbol x}) = t f({\boldsymbol x})$
Explanation. It is homogeneous of degree two.
co-f-169 · level facile · notes
The symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ is:
  1. positive definite
  2. negative definite
  3. indefinite
  4. positive semidefinite
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = (x_1 + x_2)^2 \ge 0$, zero for $(1, -1)'$.
co-f-170 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude that a symmetric ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not positive semidefinite, what suffices?
  1. $\det({\boldsymbol Q}) = 0$
  2. A zero diagonal entry
  3. Some ${\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} < 0$
  4. A negative off-diagonal entry
Explanation. The definition asks ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$.
co-f-171 · level facile · notes
The function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2x_1^2 + x_2^2$ is convex because:
  1. it is a sum of two variables
  2. ${\boldsymbol Q} = \mathrm{diag}(2, 1)$ is positive semidefinite
  3. the coefficients are integer
  4. $f({\boldsymbol 0}) = 0$
Explanation. Quadratic with positive semidefinite ${\boldsymbol Q}$ $\Rightarrow$ convex.
co-f-172 · level teorema · notes
What does the proposition on convexity of quadratic functions $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x}$ state?
  1. If ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, $f$ is convex on $\R^n$
  2. If ${\boldsymbol Q}$ has positive entries, $f$ is convex
  3. $f$ is always convex
  4. $f$ is convex only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is diagonal
Explanation. The converse also holds (next proposition).
co-f-173 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof that positive semidefinite ${\boldsymbol Q}$ makes ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x}$ convex, one reaches $\lambda(1-\lambda)({\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w})'{\boldsymbol Q}({\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w}) \ge 0$. What is needed?
  1. ${\boldsymbol p} = {\boldsymbol w}$
  2. Invertible ${\boldsymbol Q}$
  3. $\lambda > 1$
  4. Positive semidefinite ${\boldsymbol Q}$
Explanation. The factor $\lambda(1-\lambda)$ is nonnegative and so is the quadratic form.
co-f-174 · level facile · notes
Which quadratic function is concave on $\R^2$?
  1. $x_1^2 + x_2^2$
  2. $x_1 x_2$
  3. $-x_1^2 - 3x_2^2$
  4. $x_1^2 - x_2^2$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol Q} = \mathrm{diag}(-1, -3)$ is negative semidefinite.
co-f-308 · level facile · notes
With ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -2 \end{pmatrix}$, how is the quadratic function ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ written?
  1. $x_1^2 - 2x_2^2$
  2. $x_1^2 + 2x_2^2$
  3. $x_1 - 2x_2$
  4. $x_1^2 - 2x_1 x_2$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = \sum_{i,j} q_{ij} x_i x_j = 1 \cdot x_1^2 + (-2) \cdot x_2^2$: the off-diagonal entries are zero, so there is no mixed term.
co-f-309 · level facile · notes
Which symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ gives $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + 2x_2 x_3 + 3x_3^2$, with ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^3$, in the form ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 2 \\ 0 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The mixed term $2x_2x_3$ is split between $q_{23}$ and $q_{32}$, which equal $1$ each; the diagonal holds the coefficients of the squares. The last matrix gives the same function but is not symmetric.
co-f-310 · level facile · notes
With ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$, what is the quadratic function ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ at the point ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $4$
  3. $2$
  4. $6$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = 2x_1^2 + 2x_1x_2 + 2x_2^2 = 2 - 2 + 2 = 2$. With $6$ the sign of the mixed term is wrong, with $4$ it is forgotten.
co-f-311 · level facile · notes
With ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$, what is the gradient $\nabla f = 2{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ of $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol Q}\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ is the first column $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, and the gradient is twice it. With $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ the factor $2$ is forgotten.
co-f-312 · level facile · notes
What is the Hessian matrix of the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 3x_1^2 - 2x_1 x_2 + x_2^2$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 6 & -2 \\ -2 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 6 & -2 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 & -2 \\ -2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The symmetric matrix is ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and the Hessian is $2{\boldsymbol Q}$, constant. Giving ${\boldsymbol Q}$ forgets the factor $2$.
co-f-313 · level facile · notes
For the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2$, what is the radius of the circle $\{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = 8 \}$?
  1. $8$
  2. $4$
  3. $64$
  4. $2\sqrt{2}$
Explanation. $x_1^2 + x_2^2 = r^2$ with $r^2 = 8$, hence $r = \sqrt{8} = 2\sqrt{2}$. The level is the square of the radius, not the radius.
co-f-314 · level facile · notes
What shape does the graph $\{ (x_1, x_2, f({\boldsymbol x})) \}$ of the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2$ have in $\R^3$?
  1. a plane through the origin
  2. a sphere centered at the origin
  3. a paraboloid with vertex at the origin
  4. a cone with vertex at the origin
Explanation. The horizontal sections are the circles $x_1^2 + x_2^2 = k$ and the vertical sections are parabolas: it is a paraboloid. The cone is the graph of $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2$.
co-f-315 · level facile · notes
A symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite if ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$. Is the zero matrix $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ positive semidefinite?
  1. only in dimension $n = 1$
  2. yes: ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = 0 \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$
  3. no: it has no positive entries
  4. no: it is not invertible
Explanation. The definition asks for $\ge 0$, not $> 0$: the zero quadratic form satisfies it. Invertibility and the sign of the entries are irrelevant.
co-f-316 · level facile · notes
Is the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + 2x_1 x_2 + x_2^2 = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$, with ${\boldsymbol Q}$ symmetric, convex?
  1. yes: $f = (x_1 + x_2)^2$ and ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite
  2. no: the mixed term $2x_1x_2$ makes it nonconvex
  3. no: the matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not invertible
  4. only where $x_1 \ge 0$ and $x_2 \ge 0$, in the first quadrant
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = (x_1 + x_2)^2 \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite (even though singular) and $f$ is convex.
co-f-317 · level facile · notes
What is the Hessian matrix of $g({\boldsymbol x}) = 2x_1^2 + x_2^2 + 4x_1 - 3$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 & 4 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 8 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The linear part $4x_1 - 3$ has zero second derivatives: the Hessian is $2{\boldsymbol Q}$ with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$.
co-f-318 · level ipotesi · notes
If the symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ is convex. With the <em>nonsymmetric</em> matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, how is the convexity of ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ studied?
  1. by looking at the diagonal of ${\boldsymbol Q}$, which is positive
  2. with the transpose $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, which is symmetric
  3. with the equivalent symmetric matrix $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. it cannot be: the proposition requires ${\boldsymbol Q}$ symmetric
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = x_1^2 + 2x_1x_2 + x_2^2$ is the same function as $\frac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol Q} + {\boldsymbol Q}') = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, which is positive semidefinite: the function is $(x_1+x_2)^2$, convex. The transpose is not symmetric.
co-f-319 · level ipotesi · notes
A symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite if ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$. Is it enough that the diagonal entries are positive? Consider ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 3 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$.
  1. no: the off-diagonal entries must be zero
  2. no: with ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ we get ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = -3$
  3. yes: a positive diagonal is always enough
  4. yes, at least for $2 \times 2$ matrices
Explanation. $x_1^2 + 6x_1x_2 + 2x_2^2$ at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ equals $1 - 6 + 2 = -3 < 0$. Nonzero off-diagonal entries are allowed: $\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$ is positive semidefinite.
co-f-320 · level ipotesi · notes
A symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite if ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$. For ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ we have ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = 2x_1x_2 \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$. Is ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite?
  1. no: with ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ it equals $-2$
  2. yes: the check for ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ is enough
  3. yes: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is symmetric
  4. no: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ has a zero diagonal
Explanation. The definition asks for ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for <em>every</em> ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n$, also with components of different signs. A zero diagonal alone is not enough to rule it out (the zero matrix is positive semidefinite).
co-f-321 · level ipotesi · notes
The proof that a positive semidefinite ${\boldsymbol Q}$ makes $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ convex reaches $\lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w}) - f(\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}) = \lambda(1-\lambda)({\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w})'{\boldsymbol Q}({\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w})$. Besides ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite, what is needed to conclude that it is $\ge 0$?
  1. that ${\boldsymbol p} \ne {\boldsymbol w}$, two distinct points
  2. that ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is also invertible
  3. that ${\boldsymbol p}, {\boldsymbol w} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ componentwise
  4. that $\lambda(1-\lambda) \ge 0$, true for $\lambda \in [0,1]$
Explanation. The product of $\lambda(1-\lambda) \ge 0$ and $({\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w})'{\boldsymbol Q}({\boldsymbol p} - {\boldsymbol w}) \ge 0$ is nonnegative. With ${\boldsymbol p} = {\boldsymbol w}$ the difference is $0$, which is fine as well.
co-f-322 · level ipotesi · notes
A quadratic function is defined as $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = \sum_{i,j} q_{ij} x_i x_j$, with ${\boldsymbol Q}$ symmetric. Does the function $g({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2 + 1$ fit this definition?
  1. yes, because ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite
  2. no, because the matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not symmetric
  3. no: ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ equals $0$ at ${\boldsymbol 0}$, while $g({\boldsymbol 0}) = 1$
  4. yes, with ${\boldsymbol Q}$ equal to the identity
Explanation. Every ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ vanishes at the origin: $g$ has an extra constant and is a quadratic function with a linear part (section 4.4), ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$ with $d = 1$.
co-f-323 · level teorema · notes
The quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite. Which statement on <em>concavity</em> follows?
  1. no quadratic function is concave
  2. $f$ is concave if and only if $-{\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite
  3. $f$ is concave if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite
  4. $f$ is concave if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ has a negative entry
Explanation. $f$ is concave if and only if $-f = {\boldsymbol x}'(-{\boldsymbol Q})\,{\boldsymbol x}$ is convex, that is, if $-{\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite; for example $-x_1^2 - x_2^2$ is concave.
co-f-337 · level teorema · notes
For every symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$, which statement on the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ is correct?
  1. if $f$ is convex, then every entry of ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is $\ge 0$
  2. if every entry of ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is $\ge 0$, then $f$ is convex
  3. if $f$ is convex, then ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is invertible
  4. if $f$ is convex, then $f({\boldsymbol x}) \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$
Explanation. If $f$ is convex, ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, that is, ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $(x_1 - x_2)^2$, convex with negative entries and not invertible; $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ has entries $\ge 0$ but $2x_1x_2$ is not convex.
co-f-338 · level teorema · notes
Let ${\boldsymbol Q} \in \R^{n \times n}$ be symmetric, ${\boldsymbol c} \in \R^n$ and $d \in \R$. Which statement on $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$ is correct?
  1. $g$ is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite and $d \ge 0$
  2. $g$ is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$
  3. $g$ is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite, for every ${\boldsymbol c}$ and $d$
  4. $g$ is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite and ${\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. The part ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$ is both convex and concave: adding it does not change convexity, which depends only on ${\boldsymbol Q}$.
co-f-339 · level teorema · notes
A symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} = (q_{ij})$ is positive semidefinite if ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x}$. Which statement holds for every positive semidefinite matrix?
  1. the off-diagonal entries are zero
  2. every diagonal entry $q_{ii}$ is $\ge 0$
  3. every entry $q_{ij}$ is $\ge 0$
  4. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is invertible
Explanation. With ${\boldsymbol x}$ equal to the $i$-th vector of the standard basis, ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} = q_{ii} \ge 0$. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ is positive semidefinite with negative off-diagonal entries and is not invertible.
co-f-389 · level facile · notes
Which point lies on the level set $\{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : 2x_1^2 - 2x_1x_2 + 2x_2^2 = 2 \}$ of the quadratic function ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{2} \\ \frac{1}{2} \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $2 - 2 + 2 = 2$ at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$; at the other points the function equals $6$, $8$ and $\frac{1}{2}$. The level set is an ellipse stretched along the diagonal $x_1 = x_2$.
co-f-390 · level facile · notes
What shape does the graph of the nonconvex quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 - x_2^2$ have?
  1. a paraboloid opening downward
  2. a cone with vertex at the origin
  3. a saddle surface
  4. a paraboloid opening upward
Explanation. Along $x_2 = 0$ the graph is the parabola $x_1^2$, along $x_1 = 0$ the parabola $-x_2^2$: it is a saddle, and its nonempty level sets with $k \ne 0$ are hyperbolas.
co-f-391 · level facile · notes
Completing the square, how is $g({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2x_1$ written?
  1. $(x_1 - 1)^2 + x_2^2 + 1$
  2. $(x_1 - 1)^2 + x_2^2 - 1$
  3. $(x_1 - 2)^2 + x_2^2 - 4$
  4. $(x_1 + 1)^2 + x_2^2 - 1$
Explanation. $x_1^2 - 2x_1 = (x_1 - 1)^2 - 1$: halve the coefficient of $x_1$ and subtract its square. The minimum of $g$ is $-1$, at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$.
co-f-392 · level facile · notes
What does $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$ become if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is the zero matrix?
  1. linear plus constant: convex and concave
  2. a nonconvex quadratic function
  3. a convex but not concave function
  4. a function undefined at some points
Explanation. What remains is ${\boldsymbol c}'{\boldsymbol x} + d$, which satisfies the convexity definition with equality. Consistent with the criterion: the zero matrix is positive semidefinite, and so is its opposite.
co-f-393 · level facile · notes
For any symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$, what is the gradient $2{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ of the quadratic function ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ at the point ${\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol 0}$?
  1. the matrix $2{\boldsymbol Q}$
  2. the vector of the diagonal entries of ${\boldsymbol Q}$
  3. it depends on the matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$
  4. the zero vector ${\boldsymbol 0}$
Explanation. $2{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol 0} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ for every ${\boldsymbol Q}$: the origin always makes the gradient of ${\boldsymbol x}'{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x}$ vanish. Whether it is a minimum depends on ${\boldsymbol Q}$ instead. $2{\boldsymbol Q}$ is the Hessian matrix.

Part: Optimization problems (171)

5. Optimization problems (91)

co-f-099 · level facile · notes
In an optimization problem with feasible region $F$ and objective function $f$, what is a point ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$ called?
  1. optimal value
  2. feasible solution
  3. optimal solution
  4. point of local minimum
Explanation. The points of $F$ are the feasible solutions; an optimal solution is a feasible solution with the best value of $f$; the optimal value $z(P)$ is a number, not a point.
co-f-100 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 2$
Explanation. For $x \in [-1,2]$ we have $|x| \le 2$ and $x^2 \le 4 = f(2)$; $-2$ would give the same value but it is not feasible. $(0)$ is the optimal solution of the minimization form.
co-f-101 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem $P$ in minimization form, whose objective function decreases indefinitely over the feasible region, what is it called and what is its optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. infeasible, with $z(P)$ not defined
  2. with an unattainable infimum, with $z(P) = 0$
  3. unbounded, with $z(P) = +\infty$
  4. unbounded, with $z(P) = -\infty$
Explanation. In minimization form an unbounded problem has $z(P) = -\infty$; $+\infty$ corresponds to the maximization form. Infeasible means $F = \emptyset$.
co-f-102 · level facile · notes
What can be said about the problem $P$: $\min\{\, \frac{1}{x} \ \text{subject to}\ x > 0 \,\}$, with optimal value denoted by $z(P)$?
  1. it is unbounded, with $z(P) = -\infty$
  2. it is infeasible
  3. it has an unattainable infimum $0$: there is no optimal solution and $z(P)$ is not defined
  4. $z(P) = 0$, attained for $x \to +\infty$
Explanation. For every $x > 0$ the point $2x$ is feasible and $\frac{1}{2x} < \frac{1}{x}$: no solution is optimal, but the values stay positive (Example 5.6).
co-f-103 · level facile · notes
What can be said about the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x \ \text{subject to}\ x^2 \le -4 \,\}$, with feasible region $F$ and optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. it has an unattainable infimum
  2. it is infeasible: $F = \emptyset$ and $z(P)$ is not defined
  3. it is unbounded, with $z(P) = -\infty$
  4. $z(P) = -2$
Explanation. $x^2 \ge 0 > -4$ for every $x$: no point satisfies the constraint (Example 5.4).
co-f-104 · level facile · notes
In an optimization problem in minimization form, with objective function $f$ and optimal value $z(P)$, what does a feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ provide?
  1. the upper bound $z(P) \le f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$
  2. the lower bound $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le z(P)$
  3. the optimal value $z(P) = f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$
  4. no information on $z(P)$
Explanation. The minimum cannot exceed the value of a feasible solution. In maximization form the opposite holds: $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$ is a lower bound.
co-f-105 · level facile · notes
In a problem in minimization form, three feasible solutions have objective function values $7$, $3$ and $5$. What is the best bound on the optimal value $z(P)$ that follows?
  1. $z(P) \le 7$
  2. $3 \le z(P)$
  3. $z(P) = 3$
  4. $z(P) \le 3$
Explanation. Every value is an upper bound; the best one is the smallest. We cannot conclude $z(P) = 3$: better solutions may exist.
co-f-106 · level facile · notes
What is the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^2 + 5 \ \text{subject to}\ -2 \le x \le 3 \,\}$?
  1. $0$
  2. $9$
  3. $5$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $\min\{a + f\} = a + \min\{f\}$ and the minimum of $x^2$ over $[-2,3]$ is $0$, at $x = 0$. $14 = 9 + 5$ is the maximum.
co-f-107 · level facile · notes
What is the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -3x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$?
  1. $12$
  2. $-12$
  3. $0$
  4. $-3$
Explanation. $\min\{-3 f\} = -3 \max\{f\}$ and the maximum of $x^2$ over $[-1,2]$ is $4$: $-12$, at $x = 2$. Using the minimum of $x^2$ would give $0$.
co-f-108 · level facile · notes
Which equality holds for every objective function $f$ and feasible region $F$ (optima taken over ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$, when they exist)?
  1. $\min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\} = -\max\{-f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  2. $\min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\} = \max\{-f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  3. $\min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\} = -\min\{-f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  4. $\max\{f({\boldsymbol x})\} = \max\{-f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
Explanation. Property (5.1): a point minimizes $f$ over $F$ if and only if it maximizes $-f$, and the values change sign.
co-f-109 · level facile · notes
Which point belongs to the spherical neighborhood $N\left(\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}, 2\right)$ with center $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $2$, that is, the set of the points at Euclidean distance <em>less</em> than $2$ from the center?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ \frac{7}{2} \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $(2,2)$ has distance $\sqrt{2} < 2$. $(3,1)$ has distance exactly $2$: the neighborhood is open and does not contain the circle. The others are at distance $2\sqrt{2}$ and $\frac{5}{2}$.
co-f-110 · level facile · notes
Let $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ and let $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$ be the spherical neighborhood with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and radius $\delta$. When is $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in D$ a point of local minimum of $f$ in $D$?
  1. if $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) < f({\boldsymbol x})$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in D$ different from $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  2. if the derivative of $f$ vanishes at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  3. if there exists $\delta > 0$ with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) \cap D$
  4. if for every $\delta > 0$ there exists ${\boldsymbol x} \in N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) \cap D$ with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$
Explanation. Definition 5.5: one radius $\delta$ for which the inequality holds in the whole neighborhood (intersected with $D$) is enough. The strict inequality over the whole $D$ describes a unique point of (global) minimum.
co-f-111 · level facile · notes
In an optimization problem with objective function $f$ and feasible region $F$, what does the operator $\argmin\{ f({\boldsymbol x}) \text{ subject to } {\boldsymbol x} \in F \}$ return?
  1. the set of all the feasible solutions
  2. the set of all the optimal solutions (empty if none exists)
  3. the optimal value $z(P)$
  4. a single optimal solution, chosen at random
Explanation. A problem may have several optimal solutions (even infinitely many, as in Example 5.2) or none: $\argmin$ returns all of them, or the empty set.
co-f-112 · level media · notes
For $f(x) = \min\{(x+2)^2,\; (x-2)^2 + 1\}$ in $[-4,4]$, which point is a point of local minimum but not of (global) minimum?
  1. $x = 2$
  2. $x = -2$
  3. $x = 4$
  4. $x = \frac{1}{8}$
Explanation. In $(1,3)$ we have $f(x) = (x-2)^2 + 1 \ge 1 = f(2)$, but $f(-2) = 0 < 1$. $x = -2$ is the global minimum, $x = 4$ the global maximum, $x = \frac{1}{8}$ a local maximum (Example 5.14).
co-f-113 · level media · notes
We optimize $f(x) = x^2$ over the feasible region $F = (0, 1]$, in minimization and in maximization form (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value). Which statement is correct?
  1. in minimization form $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$ and $z(P) = 0$; in maximization form $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$ and $z(P) = 1$
  2. in minimization form the problem is unbounded; in maximization form $z(P) = 1$
  3. in both forms the extremum is not attained
  4. in minimization form the infimum $0$ is not attained; in maximization form $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$ and $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. $0 \notin F$ and for every $x \in F$ we have $f(\frac{x}{2}) < f(x)$; the maximum instead is attained at $1 \in F$ (Example 5.5).
co-f-114 · level media · notes
For the objective function $f(x) = \max\{0,\; |x| - 1\}$ over the feasible region $F = [-3,3]$, what are the sets of optimal solutions $\argmin$ and $\argmax$?
  1. $\argmin = [-1,1]$ and $\argmax = \{3\}$
  2. $\argmin = \{0\}$ and $\argmax = \{-3, 3\}$
  3. $\argmin = [-1,1]$ and $\argmax = \{-3, 3\}$
  4. $\argmin = \{0\}$ and $\argmax = \{3\}$
Explanation. $f = 0$ on the whole $[-1,1]$ (infinitely many optimal solutions) and $f = 2$ only at $\pm 3$ (two optimal solutions), Example 5.2.
co-f-115 · level teorema · notes
On the points of local minimum and of (global) minimum of a function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$, which statement is correct?
  1. a point of local minimum cannot be a point of (global) minimum
  2. every point of (global) minimum is a point of local minimum, but the converse does not always hold
  3. every point of local minimum is a point of (global) minimum
  4. a point of (global) minimum, if it exists, is unique
Explanation. A point of minimum satisfies the inequality in the whole $D$, hence in every neighborhood. The converse is false: in Example 5.14 $x = 2$ is a local but not global minimum.
co-f-116 · level teorema · notes
For every objective function $f$, feasible region $F$ (optima taken over ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$, when they exist) and constant $a < 0$, which property is correct?
  1. $\min\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a\, \max\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  2. $\min\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a\, \min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  3. $\min\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = -a\, \max\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  4. $\min\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a + \min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
Explanation. Property (5.4): multiplying by a negative constant turns the graph upside down, and the points of minimum of $a f$ are the points of maximum of $f$.
co-f-117 · level teorema · notes
Let $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ be a feasible solution of a problem in maximization form, with objective function $f$ and optimal value $z(P)$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $z(P) \le f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$: it is an upper bound
  2. $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = z(P)$
  3. $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \ge z(P)$ only if $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is a point of local maximum
  4. $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le z(P)$: it is a lower bound
Explanation. The maximum is at least the value of every feasible solution. Upper bounds from feasible solutions arise in minimization form.
co-f-118 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ and $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$ the spherical neighborhood with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and radius $\delta$. To prove with the definition that $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is a point of local minimum of $f$ in $D$, what is enough?
  1. checking that the derivative vanishes at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  2. checking that $f$ is convex
  3. exhibiting one radius $\delta > 0$ for which $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ in $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) \cap D$
  4. checking the inequality for every $\delta > 0$
Explanation. One radius is enough: every smaller radius then works too (for $x_1^2 + x_2^2 - \frac{1}{2}x_1^2x_2^2$ at $(0,0)$ we use $\delta = 1$). Zero derivative and convexity are not the definition.
co-f-119 · level ipotesi · notes
To conclude that the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 < x \le 1 \,\}$, with $f(x) = x^2$ and $F = (0,1]$, has no optimal solution, what is used?
  1. the fact that $f$ is convex and $F$ is an interval
  2. for every $x \in F$ the point $\frac{x}{2}$ is feasible and $f(\frac{x}{2}) < f(x)$
  3. the continuity of $f$ over $F$, which has no isolated points
  4. the fact that $F$ is bounded but does not contain $0$
Explanation. Every feasible solution is beaten by another feasible solution, so none is optimal. Continuity, boundedness and convexity hold, but they do not rule out an optimal solution.
co-f-120 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that the problem $P$: $\max\{\, (x-2)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in \R \,\}$ is unbounded, what is needed?
  1. for every $M \ge 0$ a feasible solution with value greater than $M$, for instance $x = M + 3$
  2. that $f$ is convex, and hence increases moving away from the vertex $x = 2$
  3. that the derivative $2(x-2)$ vanishes at no point $x \ne 2$
  4. one feasible solution with positive value, for instance $x = 3$ with $f(3) = 1$
Explanation. Unbounded in maximization form means that $f$ increases indefinitely: $f(M+3) = (M+1)^2 > M$ for every $M \ge 0$. A single solution, convexity or the derivative are not enough.
co-f-121 · level facile · notes
Given $F \subseteq D \subseteq \R^n$ and $f : D \to \R$, what does the optimization problem $P$ in minimization form, with feasible region $F$ and objective function $f$, ask for?
  1. a point $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) \in D$ with $f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in D$
  2. a point $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) \in F$ with $f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for some ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$
  3. a point $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) \in F$ with $f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)) \le 0$, that is, of nonpositive value
  4. a point $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) \in F$ with $f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$
Explanation. We look for a point of the feasible region $F$ (not of the whole domain $D$) whose value is not beaten, downward, by any other point of $F$.
co-f-122 · level facile · notes
What is the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, 2\left(\frac{1}{5}x^2 - 3\right) \ \text{subject to}\ -5 \le x \le 5 \,\}$?
  1. $4$
  2. $0$
  3. $-6$
  4. $-3$
Explanation. With $a = 2 > 0$, $\min\{a f\} = a \min\{f\}$; $\frac{1}{5}x^2 - 3 \ge -3$, with equality at $x = 0$, so the minimum is $2\cdot(-3) = -6$. $-3$ forgets the factor $2$, $4$ is the maximum (at $x = \pm 5$).
co-f-123 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that ${\boldsymbol 0}$ is a point of local minimum of $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2 - \frac{1}{2}x_1^2x_2^2$ in $\R^2$, we use the spherical neighborhood $N({\boldsymbol 0}, 1)$ (center ${\boldsymbol 0}$, radius $1$). What is needed?
  1. the vanishing of the gradient of $f$ at ${\boldsymbol 0}$, which alone guarantees the local minimum
  2. in $N({\boldsymbol 0}, 1)$ we have $x_2^2 < 1$, hence $f({\boldsymbol x}) - f({\boldsymbol 0}) = x_1^2(1 - \frac{x_2^2}{2}) + x_2^2 \ge 0$
  3. the convexity of $f$ in the whole $\R^2$, which makes every point with zero gradient a minimum
  4. the fact that $f({\boldsymbol x}) \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$, that is, that ${\boldsymbol 0}$ is a global minimum
Explanation. In the neighborhood $1 - \frac{x_2^2}{2} > \frac{1}{2}$, so the difference is a sum of nonnegative terms. $f$ is neither convex nor $\ge 0$ everywhere: at $(3,3)$ it equals $18 - \frac{81}{2} < 0$; the zero gradient alone is not enough.

  3. yes, but only as a solution in minimization form
  4. yes: $f(3) = 2 \ge f(x)$ for every $x \in [0, 3]$

co-f-211 · level facile · notes
