co-f-212 · level facile · notes
In an optimization problem with objective function $f$ and feasible region $F$ that has <em>no</em> optimal solutions, what does $\argmax\{ f({\boldsymbol x}) \text{ subject to } {\boldsymbol x} \in F \}$ return?
  1. the empty set
  2. the value $+\infty$
  3. the supremum of $f$ over $F$
  4. the whole feasible region $F$
Explanation. $\argmax$ returns the set of the optimal solutions: if there are none, it is the empty set. A value (such as $+\infty$ or the supremum) is not a set of points.
co-f-213 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem $P$ is written $\min\{ f({\boldsymbol x}) \text{ subject to } {\boldsymbol x} \in F \}$, with optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ and optimal value $z(P)$. What is its goal?
  1. computing only the optimal value $z(P)$
  2. listing all the points of the region $F$
  3. proving that the region $F$ is convex
  4. finding an optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$, when one exists
Explanation. The symbol $\min$ is an abuse of notation: we look for a point $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ of $F$, and its value $z(P) = f(\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P))$ follows.
co-f-214 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. $x^2 \ge 0 = f(0)$ for every $x$, and $0 \in [-1, 2]$: the minimum is at the interior point $x = 0$, not at an endpoint.
co-f-215 · level facile · notes
How many optimal solutions does the problem $P$: $\min\{\, \max\{0,\; x - 2\} \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ have?
  1. none, because the minimum is not unique
  2. infinitely many: every $x \in [0, 2]$
  3. only one: the point $x = 2$
  4. only one: the point $x = 0$
Explanation. $\max\{0, x-2\} = 0$ for $x \le 2$ and $> 0$ for $x > 2$: the optimal value $0$ is attained at every point of $[0,2]$. A problem can have infinitely many optimal solutions.
co-f-216 · level facile · notes
What can be said of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in \R \,\}$, with optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. it is unbounded, with $z(P) = -\infty$
  2. it has optimal solution $x = 0$, with $z(P) = 0$
  3. it is infeasible and $z(P)$ is not defined
  4. it has an unattainable infimum $0$
Explanation. $-x^2$ decreases indefinitely: for every $M$, the point $x = |M| + 1$ has value less than $-|M|$. The point $x = 0$ is the <em>maximum</em> of $-x^2$.
co-f-217 · level facile · notes
What can be said of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x_1 + x_2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 \le 1,~ x_1 + x_2 \ge 3 \,\}$, with feasible region $F$ and optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. it is unbounded: $z(P) = +\infty$, no optimal solution
  2. it has optimal value $z(P) = 1$, on the line $x_1 + x_2 = 1$
  3. it has optimal value $z(P) = 3$, on the line $x_1 + x_2 = 3$
  4. it is infeasible: $F = \emptyset$, $z(P)$ undefined
Explanation. No point has $x_1 + x_2$ both $\le 1$ and $\ge 3$: the feasible region is empty and there are no feasible solutions, hence no optimal ones.
co-f-218 · level facile · notes
What can be said of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x < 2 \,\}$, with optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. it has optimal solution $x = -1$, with value $z(P) = 1$
  2. it is unbounded, with optimal value $z(P) = +\infty$
  3. it has an unattainable supremum $4$
  4. it has optimal solution $x = 2$, with value $z(P) = 4$
Explanation. For $x$ close to $2$ the values approach $4$, but $2 \notin F$: for every $x \in [1, 2)$ the point $\frac{x+2}{2}$ is feasible and better. The other values, such as $f(-1) = 1$, are smaller.
co-f-219 · level facile · notes
For which three reasons can an optimization problem have no optimal solution?
  1. the function is not differentiable, not continuous, or not linear
  2. it is unbounded, it is infeasible, or the bound is not attained
  3. it is unbounded, it is infeasible, or it has several optimal solutions
  4. the region is not convex, the function is not convex, the region is unbounded
Explanation. These are the three cases of the notes. Having several optimal solutions is not a reason for non-existence; unbounded regions or nonconvex functions do not by themselves prevent existence.
co-f-220 · level facile · notes
In an optimization problem in minimization form, with objective function $f$ and feasible region $F$, what are an optimal solution and the optimal value also called?
  1. minimum point and minimum of $f$ over $F$
  2. stationary point and critical value of $f$
  3. local minimum and infimum of $f$
  4. maximum point and maximum of $f$ over $F$
Explanation. In minimization form, an optimal solution is a (global) minimum point of $f$ in $F$ and the optimal value is the minimum of $f$ over $F$.
co-f-221 · level facile · notes
In a problem in minimization form, with objective function $f$ and optimal value $z(P)$, a feasible solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ gives the upper bound $z(P) \le f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$. If $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = z(P)$, what can be concluded?
  1. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is not feasible
  2. the problem is unbounded
  3. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is only a local minimum point
  4. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is an optimal solution
Explanation. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is feasible and its value equals the optimal value: no point of $F$ does better, so $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is optimal (like the fourth solution in the figure of the notes).
co-f-222 · level facile · notes
For the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$, with optimal value $z(P)$, what does the feasible solution $\bar x = \frac{1}{2}$ give?
  1. the upper bound $z(P) \le \frac{1}{2}$
  2. the optimal value $z(P) = \frac{1}{4}$
  3. the upper bound $z(P) \le \frac{1}{4}$
  4. the lower bound $\frac{1}{4} \le z(P)$
Explanation. Its objective value is $(\frac{1}{2})^2 = \frac{1}{4}$, and in minimization form a feasible solution gives an upper bound. It is not the optimal value, which is $0$.
co-f-223 · level ipotesi · notes
In a problem in minimization form, with objective function $f$, feasible region $F$ and optimal value $z(P)$, which condition on a point $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is needed to state $z(P) \le f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$?
  1. that $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is a local minimum point
  2. that $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is feasible, that is, $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in F$
  3. that $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is an optimal solution
  4. that $f$ is convex on $F$
Explanation. $z(P) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ holds for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$, so feasibility is enough. For a point outside $F$ the value can be smaller than $z(P)$.
co-f-224 · level facile · notes
The objective function $f$ has minimum $-2$ over $F$, attained only at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$. What are the optimal solutions and the optimal value of $\min\{\, f({\boldsymbol x}) + 7 \ \text{subject to}\ {\boldsymbol x} \in F \,\}$?
  1. optimal solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $5$
  2. optimal solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $-2$
  3. optimal solution $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$, optimal value $-14$
  4. optimal solutions changed, optimal value $5$
Explanation. $\min\{a + f\} = a + \min\{f\}$: the optimal value becomes $7 + (-2) = 5$, and adding a constant shifts the graph without changing the optimal solutions.
co-f-225 · level facile · notes
Given an objective function $f$ over a feasible region $F$, when does a point of $F$ minimize $f$ over $F$?
  1. if and only if it minimizes $-f$ over $F$
  2. if and only if it maximizes $f$ over $F$
  3. if and only if $f$ is zero there
  4. if and only if it maximizes $-f$ over $F$
Explanation. From $\min\{f\} = -\max\{-f\}$: changing sign swaps minimum and maximum, and the optimal solutions are the same.
co-f-226 · level facile · notes
Over a feasible region $F$, the objective function $f$ has maximum $3$, attained only at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$, and minimum $-1$, attained only at $\hat{\boldsymbol x}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of $\min\{\, -2 f({\boldsymbol x}) \ \text{subject to}\ {\boldsymbol x} \in F \,\}$?
  1. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$, with optimal value $6$
  2. $\hat{\boldsymbol x}$, with optimal value $-2$
  3. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$, with optimal value $-6$
  4. $\hat{\boldsymbol x}$, with optimal value $2$
Explanation. With $a = -2 < 0$, $\min\{a f\} = a \max\{f\} = -2 \cdot 3 = -6$, attained where $f$ is largest, that is, at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$.
co-f-227 · level facile · notes
What happens if the objective function $f$ of a minimization problem over a region $F$ is multiplied by a constant $a > 0$?
  1. every minimum point becomes a maximum point
  2. the optimal value is multiplied by $a$, the solutions stay the same
  3. the optimal solutions change, the optimal value stays the same
  4. nothing changes, not even the optimal value
Explanation. $\min\{a f\} = a \min\{f\}$ for $a > 0$: the graph is stretched vertically and the minimum points stay the same. Minimum and maximum are swapped when $a < 0$.
co-f-228 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = |x - 2|$ on the domain $D = [0, 5]$, what are the (global) maximum point and the maximum of $f$ in $D$?
  1. $\tilde x = 5$, maximum $3$
  2. $\tilde x = 0$, maximum $2$
  3. $\tilde x = 2$, maximum $0$
  4. $\tilde x = 5$, maximum $5$
Explanation. $|x - 2|$ is the distance from $2$: in $[0,5]$ the farthest point is $5$, with $f(5) = 3 > 2 = f(0)$. The point $2$ is the minimum point.
co-f-229 · level facile · notes
What else is a (global) minimum point $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}$ of a function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ in $D$ called?
  1. maximizer of $f$ in $D$
  2. stationary point of $f$
  3. infimum of $f$ in $D$
  4. minimizer of $f$ in $D$
Explanation. The notes call a minimum point a minimizer (and a maximum point a maximizer). The infimum is a value, not a point.
co-f-230 · level facile · notes
In $\R$, what is the spherical neighborhood $N\big((3), 2\big)$ with center $3$ and radius $2$, that is, the set of the points at distance less than $2$ from $3$?
  1. the interval $(1, 5]$
  2. the open interval $(3, 5)$
  3. the open interval $(1, 5)$
  4. the closed interval $[1, 5]$
Explanation. $|x - 3| < 2$ is equivalent to $1 < x < 5$: in $\R$ the neighborhood is an open interval centered at the center, without endpoints.
co-f-231 · level facile · notes
Does the point ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$ belong to the spherical neighborhood $N\left(\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}, 1\right)$, the set of the points at Euclidean distance less than $1$ from $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. no: its distance from the center is $2$
  2. no: its distance from the center is exactly $1$
  3. yes: its distance from the center is $1 \le 1$
  4. yes: the neighborhood contains its bounding circle
Explanation. $\sqrt{(4-4)^2 + (4-5)^2} = 1$, which is not less than $1$: the point lies on the circle, excluded from the neighborhood (strict inequality).
co-f-232 · level facile · notes
How many local minimum points can a function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ have in $D$?
  1. even infinitely many
  2. at most one
  3. at most two
  4. exactly one, if the minimum exists
Explanation. For example $f(x) = 0$ on $\R$: every point is a local (and global) minimum point. The notes observe it for local minima and for minimum points.
co-f-233 · level facile · notes
For $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2 - \frac{1}{2}x_1^2 x_2^2$ on $\R^2$, the point ${\boldsymbol 0}$ is a local minimum point with $f({\boldsymbol 0}) = 0$. Why is it not a (global) minimum point?
  1. because $f$ is unbounded above
  2. because the gradient does not vanish at ${\boldsymbol 0}$
  3. because $f\left(\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}\right) = 0$
  4. because $f\left(\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}\right) = -\frac{45}{2} < 0$
Explanation. One point with value less than $f({\boldsymbol 0}) = 0$ is enough: $9 + 9 - \frac{81}{2} = -\frac{45}{2}$. That $f$ grows elsewhere does not matter; at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ it equals $\frac{3}{2}$.
co-f-234 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = \min\{(x+2)^2,\; (x-2)^2 + 1\}$ on the domain $[-4, 4]$, what are the (global) maximum point and the maximum?
  1. $x = \frac{1}{8}$, maximum $\frac{289}{64}$
  2. $x = 2$, maximum $1$
  3. $x = 4$, maximum $5$
  4. $x = -4$, maximum $4$
Explanation. $f(4) = \min\{36, 5\} = 5$, $f(-4) = \min\{4, 37\} = 4$, $f(\frac{1}{8}) = \frac{289}{64} < 5$: the last two are local maxima, not global; $x = 2$ is a local minimum.
co-f-235 · level teorema · notes
Let $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ and $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$ the spherical neighborhood with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and radius $\delta$. If $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ for all ${\boldsymbol x} \in N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) \cap D$, which statement is correct?
  1. $f$ is constant on the whole neighborhood $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) \cap D$
  2. the same inequality holds with every radius smaller than $\delta$
  3. the same inequality holds with every radius larger than $\delta$
  4. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is also a (global) minimum point of $f$ on all of $D$
Explanation. A smaller neighborhood is contained in the one with radius $\delta$, so the inequality still holds; with larger radii it may fail, and the local minimum may not be global.
co-f-236 · level teorema · notes
For every objective function $f$, every feasible region $F$ and every constant $a > 0$ (optima over ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$, when they exist), which property is correct?
  1. $\max\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a\, \max\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  2. $\max\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a\, \min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  3. $\min\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = -a\, \max\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  4. $\min\{a + f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a\, \min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
Explanation. A positive constant stretches the graph without flipping it: this holds for the maximum as for the minimum. Swapping min/max is needed only with $a < 0$, and an added constant is added to the optimal value.
co-f-237 · level teorema · notes
On the optimal value $z(P)$ of an optimization problem $P$ with feasible region $F$, which statement is correct?
  1. if $F = \emptyset$ it is $+\infty$; if $P$ is a minimization and unbounded it is $-\infty$
  2. if the infimum is not attained, $z(P)$ is the infimum
  3. if $F$ is bounded, $z(P)$ is always defined
  4. if $F = \emptyset$ it is not defined; if $P$ is a minimization and unbounded it is $-\infty$
Explanation. For an infeasible problem $z(P)$ is not defined, and likewise when the bound is not attained (even with $F$ bounded, as $F = (0,1]$ for $\min x^2$); an unbounded minimization problem has $z(P) = -\infty$.
co-f-340 · level teorema · notes
In a minimization problem with objective function $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ and feasible region $F \subseteq D$, which points are the optimal solutions?
  1. the (global) minimum points of $f$ in $F$
  2. the local minimum points of $f$ in $F$
  3. the points of $F$ where the gradient of $f$ vanishes
  4. the minimum points of $f$ in the domain $D$
Explanation. The optimal solutions are the global optima of $f$ restricted to $F$. A local minimum may not be global; a minimum point in $D$ may not lie in $F$; a zero gradient is neither sufficient nor necessary.
co-f-341 · level teorema · notes
For every objective function $f$, every feasible region $F$ and every constant $a < 0$ (optima over ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$, when they exist), which property is correct?
  1. $\max\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a\, \max\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  2. $\max\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = -a\, \min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
  3. $\max\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = \min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\} - a$
  4. $\max\{a\, f({\boldsymbol x})\} = a\, \min\{f({\boldsymbol x})\}$
Explanation. With $a < 0$ the graph flips: the maximum of $a f$ occurs where $f$ is smallest, and equals $a$ times the minimum of $f$ (for example $\max\{-x^2\} = -\min\{x^2\}$).
co-f-342 · level facile · notes
In an optimization problem $P$ in maximization form, with objective function $f$ and feasible region $F$, what does it mean that the optimal value is $z(P) = +\infty$?
  1. that the supremum is attained at infinity
  2. that the optimal solution is the point $x = +\infty$
  3. that the problem is unbounded: $f$ grows indefinitely over $F$
  4. that the feasible region $F$ is the empty set
Explanation. $z(P) = +\infty$ is the convention for an unbounded maximization problem; there is no optimal solution. For $F = \emptyset$ the optimal value is not defined.
co-f-343 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x-2)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in \R \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. it has an unattainable infimum $0$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 0$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 4$
  4. it is unbounded, with $z(P) = -\infty$
Explanation. $(x-2)^2 \ge 0 = f(2)$ for every $x$: the minimum exists even though $F = \R$ is unbounded. It is the <em>maximization</em> form of the same problem that is unbounded.
co-f-344 · level facile · notes
What can be said of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, \frac{1}{x} \ \text{subject to}\ x > 0 \,\}$, with optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. it is unbounded, with $z(P) = +\infty$
  2. it has a supremum not attained at $x = 0$
  3. it has optimal solution $x = 1$, with $z(P) = 1$
  4. it is infeasible, because $x = 0$ is excluded
Explanation. For every $M > 0$ the point $x = \frac{1}{M+1}$ is feasible and has value $M + 1 > M$: the objective function grows indefinitely near $0$. There is no finite supremum.
co-f-345 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 < x \le 1 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. it does not exist: the supremum $1$ is not attained
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 0$
  3. it is unbounded, with $z(P) = +\infty$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. $x^2 \le 1 = f(1)$ for $0 < x \le 1$ and $1 \in F$: the maximum is attained. The excluded endpoint, $0$, matters for the minimization form, which has an unattainable infimum.
co-f-346 · level facile · notes
In the definition of an optimization problem a feasible region $F$ and an objective function $f$ with domain $D$ are given. Which relation must hold between $F$ and $D$?
  1. $F = D$: the feasible region is the domain
  2. $F \cap D = \emptyset$: the two sets are disjoint
  3. $F \subseteq D$: $f$ is defined at every feasible point
  4. $D \subseteq F$: every point of the domain is feasible
Explanation. We must be able to compute $f({\boldsymbol x})$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$. The domain can be larger, as $D = \R$ and $F = [-1, 2]$ in the examples.
co-f-347 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem $P$ with objective function $f$ has a feasible region made of a single point, $F = \{\bar{\boldsymbol x}\}$. What can be said of the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ and of the optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is optimal only if $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 0$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and $z(P) = f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$, in minimization and maximization form
  3. they do not exist, because there are no other points to compare
  4. they exist only in minimization form, not in maximization
Explanation. The condition $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$ (or $\ge$) for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$ needs checking only for ${\boldsymbol x} = \bar{\boldsymbol x}$, where it holds with equality.
co-f-348 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, -\big(\frac{1}{5}x^2 + 1\big) \ \text{subject to}\ -5 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = -1$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = -6$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 1$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = 6$
Explanation. $\max\{-f\} = -\min\{f\}$: the minimum of $\frac{1}{5}x^2 + 1$ is $1$, at $x = 0$, hence the maximum of $-f$ is $-1$, at the same point.
co-f-349 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = |x|$ on the domain $D = [-1, 2]$, what is the point $x = 2$?
  1. only a local maximum point, not a global one
  2. a local minimum point of $f$ in $D$
  3. neither, because it is an endpoint of $D$
  4. a global (and local) maximum point
Explanation. $|x| \le 2 = f(2)$ for every $x \in [-1, 2]$: it is the maximum, and every maximum point is also a local maximum point. The endpoints of the domain can be optimal; $x = -1$ is a local maximum that is not global.
co-f-350 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = x$ on the domain $D = [0, 1]$, is the point $x = 0$ a local minimum point, that is, is there $\delta > 0$ with $f(0) \le f(x)$ for all $x$ of the neighborhood $N\big((0), \delta\big) = (-\delta, \delta)$ lying in $D$?
  1. no: an endpoint of the domain cannot be a local minimum
  2. yes, but it is only local and not global
  3. yes, even though the derivative of $f$ at $0$ is not zero
  4. no: the derivative of $f$ at $0$ equals $1 \ne 0$
Explanation. On $N((0), \delta) \cap [0,1] = [0, \delta)$ we have $f(x) = x \ge 0 = f(0)$. The definition compares values in $D$: a zero derivative is not required, and here $0$ is also the global minimum point.
co-f-351 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = \min\{(x+2)^2,\; (x-2)^2 + 1\}$ on the domain $[-4, 4]$, the (global) maximum point is $x = 4$. Which other points are local maximum points?
  1. $x = 0$ and $x = -4$
  2. $x = -4$ and $x = \frac{1}{8}$
  3. none: there is only $x = 4$
  4. $x = -2$ and $x = 2$
Explanation. Near $-4$, $f(x) = (x+2)^2 \le 4 = f(-4)$; at $x = \frac{1}{8}$ the two parabolas meet and $f$ has a peak there, $\frac{289}{64}$. The points $-2$ and $2$ are local minimum points.
co-f-352 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = x^2 - 4x$ on the domain $D = [0, 1]$, what are the (global) minimum point and the minimum of $f$ in $D$?
  1. $\tilde x = 1$, minimum $-3$
  2. $\tilde x = 2$, minimum $-4$
  3. $\tilde x = 0$, minimum $0$
  4. $\tilde x = 1$, minimum $-4$
Explanation. $f(x) = (x-2)^2 - 4$ decreases on $[0,1]$, because $2 \notin D$: the minimum is at the endpoint $1$, with $f(1) = -3$. The vertex $x = 2$ does not belong to the domain.
co-f-364 · level facile · notes
In the problem $\max\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$ the value $x^2 = 4$ is obtained for $x = -2$ and for $x = 2$. Why is only $x = 2$ an optimal solution?
  1. because $x = -2$ is not feasible
  2. because $2$ is greater than $-2$ and we maximize
  3. because $x = -2$ is a minimum point
  4. it is not true: both are optimal solutions
Explanation. An optimal solution must be feasible: $-2 \notin [-1, 2]$. In $F$ we have $|x| \le 2$, hence $x^2 \le 4 = f(2)$.
co-f-365 · level facile · notes
Can an infeasible optimization problem, that is, one with feasible region $F = \emptyset$ (and optimal value $z(P)$), also be unbounded?
  1. yes, if the objective function is unbounded on its domain
  2. yes, but only in maximization form
  3. yes: then $z(P) = -\infty$ and $z(P)$ is not defined
  4. no: on an empty $F$ the function takes no values
Explanation. Unbounded means that the objective function improves indefinitely over the feasible region $F$: with $F$ empty there are no values to compare, and $z(P)$ is not defined.
co-f-366 · level facile · notes
For the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$, with optimal value $z(P)$, the feasible solutions $\bar x^{(1)} = -1$, $\bar x^{(2)} = \frac{1}{2}$, $\bar x^{(3)} = 1$ are known. What is the best bound on $z(P)$ that they give?
  1. $z(P) \le 1$
  2. $4 \le z(P)$
  3. $1 \le z(P)$
  4. $\frac{1}{4} \le z(P)$
Explanation. The values are $1$, $\frac{1}{4}$, $1$; in maximization form every feasible solution gives a lower bound and the best one is the largest. $z(P) = 4$ does not follow from these solutions.
co-f-367 · level facile · notes
How many and which are the optimal solutions of the problem $\min\{\, -2\big(\frac{1}{5}x^2 - 3\big) \ \text{subject to}\ -5 \le x \le 5 \,\}$?
  1. none, because the coefficient is negative
  2. two: $x = -5$ and $x = 5$, with optimal value $-4$
  3. one: $x = 0$, with optimal value $6$
  4. one: $x = 5$, with optimal value $-4$
Explanation. $\min\{-2 f\} = -2 \max\{f\}$: the maximum of $\frac{1}{5}x^2 - 3$ over $[-5,5]$ is $2$, attained at both endpoints, hence two optimal solutions with value $-4$. At $x = 0$ we have the maximum of $-2f$.
co-f-368 · level facile · notes
In $\R^2$, what shape does the spherical neighborhood $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \|\bar{\boldsymbol x} - {\boldsymbol x}\| < \delta \}$ have, with the Euclidean distance?
  1. the open disk with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and radius $\delta$
  2. a closed disk with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and radius $\delta$
  3. a square with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and side $2\delta$
  4. the circle with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ and radius $\delta$
Explanation. The strict inequality excludes the points at distance exactly $\delta$. The square would be the neighborhood with the norm $\infty$.
co-f-369 · level facile · notes
Let $f : D \subseteq \R^n \to \R$ and $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$ the spherical neighborhood with center $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in D$ and radius $\delta$. What is enough to show to conclude that $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is <em>not</em> a local minimum point?
  1. that there is ${\boldsymbol x} \in D$ with $f({\boldsymbol x}) < f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$
  2. that the derivative of $f$ at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is not zero
  3. that there are $\delta > 0$ and ${\boldsymbol x} \in N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$ with $f({\boldsymbol x}) > f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$
  4. that for every $\delta > 0$ there is ${\boldsymbol x} \in N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) \cap D$ with $f({\boldsymbol x}) < f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$
Explanation. It is the negation of the definition: no radius works. A distant point with a smaller value only rules out the global minimum; the derivative is not enough (for example $f(x) = x$ on $[0,1]$ has a local minimum at $0$).
co-f-370 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = x^3$ on the domain $\R$, what is the point $x = 0$, where the derivative vanishes?
  1. a local maximum point, because $f'(0) = 0$
  2. a global minimum point of $f$ on $\R$
  3. neither a local minimum nor a local maximum
  4. a local minimum point, because $f'(0) = 0$
Explanation. In every neighborhood $(-\delta, \delta)$ there are points with $x^3 < 0$ and points with $x^3 > 0$: neither inequality of the definition holds. A zero derivative is not enough.
co-f-371 · level facile · notes
Can a function $f : D \to \R$ have a (global) minimum point in $D$ without having a maximum point?
  1. no: minimum and maximum always exist
  2. yes: $f(x) = x^2$ on $\R$ has minimum $0$ and no maximum
  3. no: if the minimum exists the maximum exists too
  4. only if the domain $D$ is bounded
Explanation. $x^2 \ge 0 = f(0)$ on $\R$, but $x^2$ grows indefinitely: the maximization problem is unbounded. The two notions are independent.

6. Convex and concave optimization problems (80)

co-f-124 · level facile · notes
When is an optimization problem called a convex optimization problem?
  1. when the feasible region is convex and the objective function is convex over it
  2. when the objective function is convex, whatever the feasible region is
  3. when the feasible region is convex, whatever the objective function is
  4. when the feasible region is a polyhedron and the objective function is linear
Explanation. Definition 6.1: both conditions are needed. The linear case over a polyhedron is a special case, not the definition.
co-f-125 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the convex minimization problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x-2)^2 + 1 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 5$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 0$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = 10$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. The derivative $2(x-2)$ vanishes at $2$, which is feasible and a point of local minimum; in a convex minimization problem every local minimum is optimal (Proposition 6.2), hence $z(P) = f(2) = 1$.
co-f-126 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the concave maximization problem $P$: $\max\{\, -(x-3)^2 + 10 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = 6$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 0$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 10$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. The derivative $-2(x-3)$ vanishes at $3$, which is feasible and a point of local maximum; in a concave maximization problem every local maximum is optimal (Corollary 6.3), hence $z(P) = f(3) = 10$.
co-f-127 · level facile · notes
In a convex minimization problem, how many points of local minimum that are not optimal solutions can exist?
  1. it depends on the dimension $n$
  2. none
  3. at most one
  4. infinitely many, if the feasible region is unbounded
Explanation. In a convex minimization problem (convex feasible region and objective function) every point of local minimum is a point of minimum, that is, an optimal solution (Proposition 6.2), whatever the dimension and even if the region is unbounded.
co-f-128 · level facile · notes
Why is the interval $[a,b] = \{ x \in \R : g_1(x) = a - x \le 0,~ g_2(x) = x - b \le 0 \}$ a convex feasible region?
  1. because it is described by constraints $g_i(x) \le 0$ with linear, hence convex, functions $g_i$
  2. because it is bounded, so every segment between two of its points stays inside it
  3. because it is closed, that is, it contains its endpoints $a$ and $b$
  4. because it is described by constraints $g_i(x) \le 0$ with differentiable functions $g_i$
Explanation. If every $g_i$ is convex, $\{ {\boldsymbol x} : g_i({\boldsymbol x}) \le 0 \}$ is convex (Observation 3.14). Closedness, boundedness and differentiability play no role.
co-f-129 · level facile · notes
Knowing that $f(x) = 2 + 9x - 6x^2 + x^3$ is convex over $[2,4]$, what are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, 2 + 9x - 6x^2 + x^3 \ \text{subject to}\ 2 \le x \le 4 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 6$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 4$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (4)$, $z(P) = 6$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 2$
Explanation. The derivative $3(x-1)(x-3)$ vanishes at $1$ and $3$; only $3$ is feasible, and it is optimal because the problem is a convex minimization problem (Example 6.3). At the endpoints $f(2) = 4$, $f(4) = 6$.
co-f-130 · level facile · notes
Knowing that $f(x) = 2 + 9x - 6x^2 + x^3$ is concave over $[0,2]$, what are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, 2 + 9x - 6x^2 + x^3 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 2 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 2$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 2$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 6$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 4$
Explanation. The derivative $3(x-1)(x-3)$ vanishes at $1$ and $3$; only $1$ is feasible, it is a local maximum and, in the concave maximization problem, it is optimal: $z(P) = 2 + 9 - 6 + 1 = 6$.
co-f-131 · level facile · notes
Is the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x^2 \ge 1 \,\}$ a convex minimization problem?
  1. no: the objective function is concave
  2. no: the feasible region $(-\infty,-1] \cup [1,+\infty)$ is not convex
  3. yes: the objective function is convex
  4. yes: the feasible region is closed
Explanation. $-1$ and $1$ are feasible but their midpoint $0$ is not. The convexity of $x^2$ alone is not enough.
co-f-132 · level facile · notes
To solve a convex problem in one variable, such as ${\min\{\, (x-2)^2 + 1 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}}$, what role does the point where the derivative of the objective function vanishes play?
  1. it suggests the candidate, whose local optimality is then checked with the definition
  2. it proves by itself that the point is an optimal solution, for every problem
  3. it proves that the objective function is convex
  4. it is always an endpoint of the feasible region
Explanation. The zero derivative indicates where to look; then the local minimum (maximum) is checked in a neighborhood and Proposition 6.2 (or Corollary 6.3) gives optimality.
co-f-133 · level facile · notes
In the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$, which statement is correct?
  1. $x = -1$ is the optimal solution, because it is a point of local minimum
  2. $x = 0$ is the optimal solution, because the derivative vanishes there
  3. it is a convex minimization problem, hence $x = -1$ is optimal
  4. $x = -1$ is a point of local minimum but not an optimal solution, which is $x = 2$
Explanation. The objective function is concave, so it is not a convex minimization problem and local minima may not be optimal. $f(-1) = -1$ is a local minimum, but $f(2) = -4 < -1$; at $0$ there is the maximum.
co-f-134 · level facile · notes
In the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 2 \,\}$, what can be said about the point $x = -1$?
  1. it is not a point of local maximum, because $x^2$ grows toward $x = 2$
  2. the problem is a concave maximization problem, so it is optimal
  3. it is a point of local maximum but not an optimal solution, which is $x = 2$
  4. it is the optimal solution, because it is a point of local maximum
Explanation. Near $-1$ (inside $F$) we have $x^2 \le 1$, but $f(2) = 4 > 1$. The function is convex, not concave: the result «local maximum = optimal» holds only for concave maximization.
co-f-135 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1-2)^2 + (x_2-2)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 \le 2,~ x_1 \ge 0,~ x_2 \ge 0 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 2$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 8$
Explanation. It is the point of the triangle closest to $(2,2)$, which is not feasible: the level circle $2$ touches the side $x_1 + x_2 = 2$ at $(1,1)$ (Exercise 6.3).
co-f-136 · level media · notes
Completing the squares, how can the function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 4x_1 + 2x_2 - x_1^2 - x_2^2$ of ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$ be written?
  1. $5 - (x_1-2)^2 - (x_2-1)^2$
  2. $5 - (x_1+2)^2 - (x_2+1)^2$
  3. $3 - (x_1-2)^2 - (x_2-1)^2$
  4. $-5 + (x_1-2)^2 + (x_2-1)^2$
Explanation. $x_1^2 - 4x_1 = (x_1-2)^2 - 4$ and $x_2^2 - 2x_2 = (x_2-1)^2 - 1$, hence $f = 5 - (x_1-2)^2 - (x_2-1)^2$ (Exercise 6.4).
co-f-137 · level media · notes
Let $F_2 = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2 \ge 0 \}$. Which computation proves that $F_2$ is not convex?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \notin F_2$, because $-2 < 0$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \in F_2$, and their sum $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ does not belong to $F_2$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix} \in F_2$, and with $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ we get $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, which does not belong to $F_2$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \in F_2$, and with $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ we get $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, where $0 + 0 - 2 = -2 < 0$
Explanation. We need a convex combination of two points of $F_2$ outside $F_2$. The midpoint $(1,1)$ of $(2,0)$ and $(0,2)$ gives $1 + 1 - 2 = 0 \ge 0$: it belongs to $F_2$, so it proves nothing (Exercise 6.5).
co-f-138 · level media · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1-3)^2 + (x_2-3)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2 \le 0 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 18$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 2$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 8$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
Explanation. It is the point of the disk of radius $\sqrt{2}$ closest to $(3,3)$, which is not feasible: along the direction $(1,1)$ we reach the boundary at $(1,1)$, with $(1-3)^2 + (1-3)^2 = 8$.
co-f-139 · level teorema · notes
On convex and concave optimization problems (convex feasible region, convex or concave objective function), which statement is correct?
  1. in a convex minimization problem, the optimal solution is always unique
  2. in a convex minimization problem, every point of local minimum is an optimal solution
  3. in a convex minimization problem, every feasible solution is a point of local minimum
  4. in a maximization problem with a convex objective function, every point of local maximum is an optimal solution
Explanation. It is Proposition 6.2. The optimal solution may not be unique ($\min\{\max\{0, |x|-1\} : -3 \le x \le 3\}$ has infinitely many optimal solutions); the maximization of a convex function is disproved by $\max\{x^2 : -1 \le x \le 2\}$.
co-f-140 · level teorema · notes
On problems with convex feasible region and concave objective function, which statement is correct?
  1. in a concave maximization problem, a point is a point of local maximum if and only if it is a point of maximum
  2. in a concave minimization problem, every point of local minimum is an optimal solution
  3. in a concave maximization problem, the optimal solution always lies on the boundary of the feasible region
  4. in a concave maximization problem, every point of local maximum is unique
Explanation. Corollary 6.3: maximizing a concave $f$ is equivalent to minimizing the convex $-f$, where every local minimum is optimal. In concave minimization the local minima may not be optimal (for instance $\min\{-x^2 : -1 \le x \le 2\}$ at $x = -1$).
co-f-141 · level teorema · notes
On the inequality of cubes, for $a, b \in \R$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$, which statement is correct?
  1. $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ for every $a, b \in \R$
  2. $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ if $a, b \le 0$
  3. $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \ge \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ if $a, b \ge 0$
  4. $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ if $a, b \ge 0$
Explanation. Observation 6.4: the difference equals $\lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2\big((1+\lambda) a + (2-\lambda) b\big)$, nonnegative if $a, b \ge 0$ and nonpositive if $a, b \le 0$.
co-f-142 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that in a convex minimization problem ($F$ convex, $f$ convex over $F$) every point of local minimum $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is optimal, we compare it with an arbitrary ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$. What is used?
  1. the boundedness of $F$, which guarantees the existence of a point of minimum
  2. the uniqueness of the optimal solution, which must then coincide with $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  3. the convexity of $F$ and of $f$, the point $\lambda {\boldsymbol x} + (1-\lambda)\bar{{\boldsymbol x}}$ close to $\bar{{\boldsymbol x}}$ for small $\lambda$, and the division by $\lambda > 0$
  4. the differentiability of $f$ and the vanishing of the derivative at $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$, which locates the minimum
Explanation. With $\hat{\boldsymbol x} = \lambda {\boldsymbol x} + (1-\lambda)\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in F$ in the neighborhood, from the two inequalities $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f(\hat{\boldsymbol x}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol x}) + (1-\lambda) f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$ we get $\lambda f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le \lambda f({\boldsymbol x})$ and divide by $\lambda > 0$. No derivatives are needed.
co-f-143 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that in a concave maximization problem ($F$ convex, $f$ concave over $F$) every point of local maximum is optimal, what is used?
  1. the fact that $f \ge 0$ over $F$, so that maximizing $f$ is the same as maximizing $|f|$
  2. $-f$ convex, a local maximum of $f$ is a local minimum of $-f$, and in convex problems local minima are optimal
  3. the differentiability of $f$ and the negative sign of the second derivative at the local maximum
  4. the boundedness of $F$ and the continuity of $f$, which guarantee the existence of a maximum
Explanation. $f$ concave is equivalent to $-f$ convex and $\max\{f\} = -\min\{-f\}$: we apply the result on local minima of convex minimization problems. Neither derivatives nor boundedness are needed.
co-f-144 · level ipotesi · notes
We want to prove that $f(x) = (x-2)^3 - 3(x-2) + 4$ is convex over $[2,4]$ with the inequality of cubes $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$, setting $a = p - 2$, $b = w - 2$ with $p, w \in [2,4]$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$. What is needed?
  1. that $a, b \ge 0$, the condition under which the inequality of cubes holds
  2. that $a, b \le 0$, the condition under which the inequality of cubes holds
  3. nothing on the sign: the inequality of cubes holds for every $a, b \in \R$
  4. the single case $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$, which then extends by symmetry
Explanation. The inequality of cubes with $\le$ holds for $a, b \ge 0$, and over $[2,4]$ we have $p - 2, w - 2 \ge 0$; for $a, b \le 0$ it holds with $\ge$ (concavity over $[0,2]$). The part $-3(x-2) + 4$ is linear and does not change convexity.
co-f-145 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the convex minimization problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x-5)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 3 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = 0$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 25$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 2$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 4$
Explanation. The derivative $2(x-5)$ vanishes at $5$, which is not feasible; over $[0,3]$ the derivative is negative, so $f$ decreases and the minimum is at the endpoint $3$, with $(3-5)^2 = 4$. $(5)$ is not feasible, $(0)$ is the maximum.
co-f-191 · level facile · notes
When is an optimization problem $\min\{f({\boldsymbol x}) : {\boldsymbol x} \in F\}$ called convex?

  2. If $F$ is convex and $f$ is convex on $F$
  3. If $f$ is convex, whatever $F$
  4. If $F$ is bounded
Explanation. A convex region and a convex objective are needed.
co-f-192 · level facile · notes
Which problem is convex?

  2. $\min\{-x_1^2 : -1 \le x_1 \le 1\}$
  3. $\min\{x_1^2 : x_1 \in \{0, 1\}\}$
  4. $\min\{x_1 : x_1^2 \ge 1\}$

co-f-193 · level teorema · notes

  3. It is concave but not convex
  4. It is both convex and concave

co-f-194 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that $\min\{\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 : {\boldsymbol A}{\boldsymbol x} = {\boldsymbol b}\}$ is a convex problem, what is needed?

co-f-196 · level facile · notes

co-f-198 · level ipotesi · notes
To derive the corollary on maxima of concave problems from the proposition on minima of convex problems, what is used?

co-f-199 · level facile · notes

co-f-200 · level facile · notes

co-f-201 · level teorema · notes

co-f-202 · level ipotesi · notes

Explanation. With nonconvex $F$ (e.g. two disjoint intervals) a local minimum may not be global.
co-f-208 · level facile · notes
For $\min\{x^2 : x \in [-2, -1] \cup [1, 3]\}$, why can the proposition «local minimum $\Rightarrow$ global» not be applied?
  1. The region is not convex
  2. $x^2$ is not convex
  3. The region is unbounded
  4. The problem has no local minima
Explanation. The union of two disjoint intervals is not convex ($x = 0$ is missing).
co-f-238 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is called convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. Can a <em>concave</em> problem be written in minimization form?
  1. no: in minimization form it is called a convex problem
  2. yes, but only if the feasible region is bounded
  3. yes: the definition concerns $F$ and $f$, not the form
  4. no: a concave problem is by definition a maximization problem
Explanation. Convex and concave describe $F$ and $f$; the form (minimization or maximization) is another choice. For example $\min\{-x^2 \text{ subject to } x \in [-1,2]\}$ is a concave problem in minimization form.
co-f-239 · level facile · notes
Why do the definitions of convex and concave optimization problems require a convex feasible region $F$?
  1. because a convex $F$ makes every objective function convex
  2. because convex and concave functions are defined on a convex domain
  3. because otherwise the feasible region would be empty
  4. because problems with nonconvex $F$ have no solutions
Explanation. The convexity inequality uses the points $\lambda{\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}$, which must lie in the domain: to speak of $f$ convex on $F$ we need $F$ convex.
co-f-240 · level facile · notes
A feasible region is often described as $F = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : g({\boldsymbol x}) \le 0 \}$. Which function $g$ guarantees that $F$ is convex?
  1. $g({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 1$
  2. $g({\boldsymbol x}) = 1 - x_1^2 - x_2^2$
  3. $g({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1 x_2 - 1$
  4. $g({\boldsymbol x}) = x_2 - x_1^2$
Explanation. If $g$ is convex, $\{g \le 0\}$ is convex: here it is the unit disk. The second gives the outside of the disk, the third the region between two branches of a hyperbola, the fourth the region below a parabola: none is convex.
co-f-241 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is called convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. What can be said of the problem $\min\{\, |x_1| + |x_2| \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + 2x_2 = 3 \,\}$?
  1. it is not convex, because $|x_1| + |x_2|$ is not differentiable
  2. it is not convex, because the feasible region is unbounded
  3. it is concave, because the constraint is an equality
  4. it is convex: the norm $1$ is convex and the line is a convex set
Explanation. $|x_1| + |x_2| = \|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1$ is a norm, hence convex; the line $x_1 + 2x_2 = 3$ is convex. Differentiability and boundedness are not part of the definition.
co-f-242 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is called convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. What can be said of the problem $\min\{\, (x-1)^3 \ \text{subject to}\ 1 \le x \le 4 \,\}$?
  1. it is concave, because the function is a cube
  2. it is not convex, because the function is increasing
  3. it is convex: $(x-1)^3$ is convex where $x \ge 1$
  4. it is not convex, because $x^3$ is not convex on $\R$
Explanation. With $a = p - 1 \ge 0$ and $b = w - 1 \ge 0$, the inequality of cubes gives $(\lambda a + (1-\lambda)b)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$: only the behavior on the feasible region matters.
co-f-243 · level facile · notes
In a convex minimization problem, with objective function $f$ convex on the convex feasible region $F$, the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} \in F$ is a local minimum point with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 3$. What holds for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$?
  1. $f({\boldsymbol x}) \le 3$
  2. $f({\boldsymbol x}) \ge 3$
  3. $f({\boldsymbol x}) \ge 3$ only near $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$
  4. $f({\boldsymbol x}) > 3$ for ${\boldsymbol x} \ne \bar{\boldsymbol x}$
Explanation. By the proposition on convex minimization problems, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is a minimum point over all of $F$. It need not be the only one: other points can have value $3$.
co-f-244 · level facile · notes
In a convex minimization problem, with objective function $f$ and convex feasible region $F$, the points $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} \ne \bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)}$ are both local minimum points. What can be said of their values?
  1. $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)}) = f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)})$, because both are optimal
  2. they can be different, as in every problem
  3. it is impossible: a convex problem has only one local minimum
  4. $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)}) < f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)})$ if $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)}$ is more interior
Explanation. Every local minimum of a convex minimization problem is an optimal solution, and all optimal solutions have value $z(P)$. Several local minima can exist (for example with $f$ constant).
co-f-245 · level facile · notes
In a concave maximization problem $P$ (objective function $f$ concave on the convex feasible region $F$), the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is a local maximum point with $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 7$. What is the optimal value $z(P)$?
  1. $z(P) \ge 7$, but it could be larger
  2. $z(P) \le 7$, but it could be smaller
  3. it cannot be said without comparing all points
  4. $z(P) = 7$
Explanation. By the corollary on concave maximization problems, a local maximum is a maximum over all of $F$: $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ is optimal and $z(P) = f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = 7$.
co-f-246 · level facile · notes
In a <em>maximization</em> problem with a <em>convex</em> objective function on a convex feasible region, is a local maximum point always an optimal solution?
  1. yes, if the feasible region is bounded
  2. no: such a problem never has local maxima
  3. no: it can be a local maximum that is not global
  4. yes, because the objective function is convex
Explanation. For example $\max\{x^2 \text{ subject to } -1 \le x \le 2\}$: $x = -1$ is a local maximum with value $1$, but the optimal solution is $x = 2$, with value $4$.
co-f-247 · level facile · notes
In the proof that, in a convex minimization problem ($F$ convex, $f$ convex on $F$), a local minimum $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ with neighborhood $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$ is optimal, one considers the points $\hat{\boldsymbol x} = \lambda{\boldsymbol x} + (1-\lambda)\bar{\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol x} \in F$. Where is $\hat{\boldsymbol x}$ for $\lambda > 0$ small enough?
  1. on the boundary of the feasible region $F$
  2. in $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta) \cap F$
  3. outside the feasible region $F$
  4. in $N({\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$, close to ${\boldsymbol x}$
Explanation. $\hat{\boldsymbol x}$ lies in $F$ because $F$ is convex, and for small $\lambda$ it is close to $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$: there $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f(\hat{\boldsymbol x})$, which with the convexity of $f$ gives $f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) \le f({\boldsymbol x})$.
co-f-248 · level facile · notes
In the convex minimization problem $\min\{\, (x-2)^2 + 1 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}$, which spherical neighborhood $N(\bar x, \delta)$ allows us to check with the definition that $x = 2$ is a local minimum point?
  1. $N\big((2), 1\big) = (1, 3)$, whose points are all feasible
  2. $N\big((2), 3\big) = (-1, 5)$, whose points are all feasible
  3. none: it is enough that the derivative vanishes at $x = 2$
  4. $N\big((0), 2\big) = (-2, 2)$, centered at the endpoint $0$
Explanation. On $(1,3) \subseteq [0,5]$ we have $f(x) = (x-2)^2 + 1 \ge 1 = f(2)$. The interval $(-1,5)$ contains infeasible points; a zero derivative only suggests the candidate.
co-f-249 · level facile · notes
In the concave maximization problem $\max\{\, -(x-3)^2 + 10 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}$, what is the derivative of the objective function and where does it vanish?
  1. $-2x - 6$, zero at $x = -3$
  2. $2x - 6$, zero at $x = 3$, which is a minimum
  3. $-x + 3$, zero at $x = 3$
  4. $-2x + 6$, zero at $x = 3$
Explanation. $\frac{d}{dx}\big(-(x-3)^2 + 10\big) = -2(x-3) = -2x + 6$: it vanishes at $x = 3$, which is feasible and is the candidate maximum point.
co-f-250 · level facile · notes
For $a, b \ge 0$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$ we have $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$. What are the two sides for $a = 1$, $b = 3$, $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$?
  1. $8 \le 8$
  2. $4 \le 14$
  3. $8 \le 14$
  4. $14 \le 8$
Explanation. $\big(\frac{1}{2} + \frac{3}{2}\big)^3 = 2^3 = 8$ and $\frac{1}{2} \cdot 1 + \frac{1}{2} \cdot 27 = 14$: with $a, b \ge 0$ the cube of the average does not exceed the average of the cubes.
co-f-251 · level facile · notes
The function $f(x) = 2 + 9x - 6x^2 + x^3$ has $f(0) = 2$, $f(1) = 6$, $f(2) = 4$. Which computation proves that $f$ is not convex on $\R$?
  1. $f(0) < f(2)$, hence $f$ is not decreasing
  2. with $p = 0$, $w = 2$, $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$: $f(1) = 6 > 3 = \frac{1}{2} f(0) + \frac{1}{2} f(2)$
  3. with $p = 2$, $w = 4$, $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$: $f(3) = 2 < 5 = \frac{1}{2} f(2) + \frac{1}{2} f(4)$
  4. the derivative $f'(x) = 3(x-1)(x-3)$ vanishes at two points
Explanation. One pair of points where the graph lies above the chord is enough: it violates the convexity inequality. The point below the chord at $x = 3$ rules out concavity instead; the zeros of the derivative or monotonicity prove nothing.
co-f-252 · level facile · notes
In the convex minimization problem $\min\{\, 2 + 9x - 6x^2 + x^3 \ \text{subject to}\ 2 \le x \le 4 \,\}$, the derivative $3(x-1)(x-3)$ vanishes at $x = 1$ and $x = 3$. Which point is the candidate?
  1. $x = 3$, because $x = 1$ is not feasible
  2. $x = 1$, because it vanishes there first
  3. both, because the derivative vanishes there
  4. the endpoint $x = 2$, because $f$ is convex
Explanation. Only $x = 3$ lies in $[2, 4]$; then the definition confirms that it is a local minimum (on $(2,4)$ we have $f(x) - 2 = x(x-3)^2 \ge 0$), hence optimal.
co-f-253 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = 2 + 9x - 6x^2 + x^3$ we have $f(x) - 2 = x(x-3)^2$. Why, on the neighborhood $N\big((3), 1\big) = (2, 4)$, do we have $f(x) \ge f(3) = 2$?
  1. because the derivative of $f$ vanishes at $x = 3$
  2. because $(x-3)^2 \ge 0$ is enough whatever the sign of $x$
  3. because $f$ is increasing on $(2, 4)$
  4. because on $(2, 4)$ we have $x > 0$, hence $x(x-3)^2 \ge 0$
Explanation. The product of $x > 0$ and $(x-3)^2 \ge 0$ is nonnegative: this is the direct comparison of the definition of local minimum. $f$ is not increasing on $(2,3)$, and for $x < 0$ the sign would change.
co-f-254 · level facile · notes
To prove that $f(x) = (x-2)^3 - 3(x-2) + 4$ is concave on $[0, 2]$ one sets $a = p - 2$ and $b = w - 2$, with $p, w \in [0,2]$. What allows the conclusion?
  1. the derivative of $f$ vanishes at $x = 1$, inside $[0, 2]$
  2. $f(0) = 2 < 6 = f(1)$, hence $f$ increases on $[0, 1]$
  3. $a, b \le 0$: the inequality of cubes holds with $\ge$
  4. $a, b \ge 0$: the inequality of cubes holds with $\le$
Explanation. For $p, w \le 2$ the numbers $a, b$ are nonpositive, and the inequality of cubes holds with $\ge$; the term $-3(x-2) + 4$ is linear, so $f$ satisfies the concavity inequality.
co-f-255 · level facile · notes
To prove with the definition that $f(x) = (x-2)^2 + 1$ is convex, which inequality do the notes use (for $a, b \in \R$ and $\lambda \in [0,1]$)?
  1. $\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b \le \max\{a, b\}$ per $\lambda \in [0,1]$
  2. $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^2 \le \lambda a^2 + (1-\lambda) b^2$
  3. $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$
  4. $|\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b| \le \lambda |a| + (1-\lambda) |b|$
Explanation. With $a = p - 2$ and $b = w - 2$ one applies the inequality of squares of the chapter «Convex and concave functions», valid for all $a, b$; the constant $1 = \lambda + (1-\lambda)$ is distributed.
co-f-256 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the convex minimization problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x-3)^2 + 1 \ \text{subject to}\ x^2 - 4 \le 0 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 2$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-2)$, $z(P) = 26$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. $x^2 - 4 \le 0$ gives $F = [-2, 2]$; the point $x = 3$, where the derivative vanishes, is not feasible, and $(x-3)^2$ decreases on all of $F$: the optimum is $x = 2$, with value $1 + 1 = 2$.
co-f-257 · level facile · notes
For $f(x) = 3 - (x-1)^2$ and $F = [-1, 4]$, what are the optimal solutions of $\min\{ f(x) \text{ subject to } x \in F \}$ and of $\max\{ f(x) \text{ subject to } x \in F \}$?
  1. minimum at $x = -1$ (value $-1$), maximum at $x = 1$ (value $3$)
  2. minimum at $x = 1$ (value $3$), maximum at $x = 4$ (value $-6$)
  3. minimum at $x = 4$ (value $-6$), maximum at $x = -1$ (value $-1$)
  4. minimum at $x = 4$ (value $-6$), maximum at $x = 1$ (value $3$)
Explanation. $f$ is concave: the maximum is at the vertex $x = 1$ (concave maximization problem). The minimum is at the endpoint farthest from $1$: $f(-1) = -1$, $f(4) = -6$; $x = -1$ is only a local minimum.
co-f-258 · level media · notes
Knowing that $4x_1 + 2x_2 - x_1^2 - x_2^2 = 5 - (x_1-2)^2 - (x_2-1)^2$, what are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, 4x_1 + 2x_2 - x_1^2 - x_2^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + 2x_2 \le 2,~ x_1 \ge 0,~ x_2 \ge 0 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{8}{5} \\ \frac{1}{5} \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = \frac{21}{5}$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
Explanation. Maximizing is equivalent to finding the feasible point closest to $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, which violates $x_1 + 2x_2 \le 2$. The closest point of the line $x_1 + 2x_2 = 2$ is $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} - \frac{2}{5}\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{8}{5} \\ \frac{1}{5} \end{pmatrix}$, which is feasible, with value $5 - \frac{4}{25} - \frac{16}{25} = \frac{21}{5}$.
co-f-259 · level teorema · notes
On a convex minimization problem (convex feasible region $F$, objective function $f$ convex on $F$), which statement is correct?
  1. every feasible point of it is a local minimum point
  2. it may have no optimal solutions, as $\min\{x \text{ subject to } x > 0\}$
  3. it always has at least one optimal solution
  4. it has at most one local minimum point
Explanation. The proposition says that <em>if</em> there is a local minimum, it is optimal; it does not guarantee existence. In the example $F = (0, +\infty)$ is convex, $f(x) = x$ is convex and the infimum $0$ is not attained.
co-f-260 · level teorema · notes
For $\lambda \in [0,1]$ we have $\lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3 - \big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 = \lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2\big((1+\lambda)a + (2-\lambda)b\big)$. What sign does this difference have if $a \ge 0 \ge b$?
  1. it can be positive, negative or zero
  2. it is always $\ge 0$, as for $a, b \ge 0$
  3. it is always $\le 0$, as for $a, b \le 0$
  4. it is always $0$, because the signs cancel
Explanation. The factor $\lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2$ is $\ge 0$, but $(1+\lambda)a + (2-\lambda)b$ changes sign: for example with $\lambda = \frac{1}{2}$ it equals $\frac{3}{2}(a + b)$. This is why the observation requires $a, b$ of the same sign.
co-f-261 · level teorema · notes
Let $F = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^n : g_i({\boldsymbol x}) \le 0, ~ i = 1, \dots, m \}$ be the feasible region of an optimization problem. Which statement is correct?
  1. if $F$ is convex, every function $g_i$ is convex
  2. if every function $g_i$ is concave, $F$ is convex
  3. $F$ is convex only if $m = 1$
  4. if every function $g_i$ is convex, $F$ is convex
Explanation. This is the property of sets defined by convex functions. The converse is false: $\{x : -x^3 \le 0\} = [0, +\infty)$ is convex with $-x^3$ not convex; with concave $g_i$ the set $F$ may not be convex.
co-f-353 · level teorema · notes
Let $f$ be convex on the convex feasible region $F$. Which statement is correct?
  1. every local maximum of $f$ in $F$ is also a maximum of $f$ in $F$
  2. $f$ has at most one local minimum point in the whole region $F$
  3. $f$ has no local maximum point at all in the region $F$
  4. local minima are minima, local maxima are not always maxima
Explanation. The proposition concerns the minima of convex problems. It fails for maxima: $\max\{x^2 \text{ subject to } -1 \le x \le 2\}$ has the local maximum $x = -1$, not global. There can be several local minima (with $f$ constant, all points).
co-f-354 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is called convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. Is the problem $\min\{\, (x-1)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\} \,\}$ a convex minimization problem?
  1. yes: the feasible region is bounded
  2. no: it is a concave problem
  3. no: the feasible region is not convex
  4. yes: the objective function is convex
Explanation. $\{0,1,2,3\}$ contains $0$ and $1$ but not $\frac{1}{2}$: it is not convex, so the problem is neither convex nor concave, even though $(x-1)^2$ is convex.
co-f-355 · level facile · notes
In the convex minimization problem $\min\{\, |x - 3| \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}$, the point $x = 3$ is a local minimum point. What can be concluded?
  1. the optimal solution is the endpoint $x = 0$
  2. it is the optimal solution, with optimal value $0$
  3. it is only a local minimum: other comparisons are needed
  4. it cannot be a local minimum, because $|x - 3|$ is not differentiable at $3$
Explanation. $|x-3|$ is convex and $[0,5]$ is convex: by the proposition the local minimum is optimal. Differentiability is not needed, neither for the definition of local minimum nor for the proposition.
co-f-356 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the concave maximization problem $P$: $\max\{\, 2 - |x| \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 3 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 2$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = -1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 1$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 0$
Explanation. $2 - |x| \le 2 = f(0)$ for every $x$, and $0 \in [-1, 3]$: it is a local maximum and hence, by the corollary, the optimal solution. The endpoints give the smallest values.
co-f-357 · level facile · notes
A local search method stops at the first local minimum point it finds. What does it guarantee, if the problem is a convex minimization problem (with optimal value $z(P)$)?
  1. that the point found is the only local minimum
  2. nothing: it may stop at a local minimum that is not global
  3. that the value found is only an upper bound on $z(P)$
  4. that the point found is an optimal solution
Explanation. In convex minimization problems every local minimum is optimal: stopping at the first one is safe. In the nonconvex example of chapter 5, instead, a method looking near $x = 2$ finds the local minimum $1$ and not $z(P) = 0$.
co-f-358 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the concave maximization problem $P$: $\max\{\, -(x-3)^2 + 10 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 2 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 1$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 10$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 9$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 10$
Explanation. The vertex $x = 3$, where the derivative vanishes, is not feasible; on $[0,2]$ the function increases, so the optimum is the closest endpoint, $x = 2$, with $-1 + 10 = 9$.
co-f-359 · level facile · notes
Let $f({\boldsymbol x}) = (x_1-3)^2 + (x_2-3)^2$, with the problems $P_1$: $\min\{ f({\boldsymbol x}) \text{ subject to } x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2 \le 0 \}$ and $P_2$: $\min\{ f({\boldsymbol x}) \text{ subject to } x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2 \ge 0 \}$. Which of the two is a convex minimization problem?
  1. neither, because the constraints are not linear
  2. only $P_1$: its region is convex, that of $P_2$ is not
  3. both, because $f$ is convex
  4. only $P_2$, because its region is unbounded
Explanation. $x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2$ is convex, so $\{\cdot \le 0\}$ (a disk) is convex; $\{\cdot \ge 0\}$ (the outside of the disk) contains $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ but not the origin.
co-f-360 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1-3)^2 + (x_2-3)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 2 \ge 0 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value), which is not convex?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 8$
  3. they do not exist, because the region is not convex
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 18$
Explanation. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ is feasible ($9 + 9 - 2 \ge 0$) and $f \ge 0 = f(3,3)$ everywhere: it is optimal. A nonconvex problem can have an optimal solution; $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ is the optimum with the constraint $\le 0$.
co-f-361 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the convex minimization problem $P$: $\min\{\, x_1^2 + x_2^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 \ge 1 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 2$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. For $x_1 \ge 1$ we have $x_1^2 + x_2^2 \ge 1 + 0 = 1$, with equality only at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$. The origin, the unconstrained minimum, is not feasible.
co-f-362 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the convex minimization problem $P$: $\min\{\, \max\{x,\; 2 - x\} \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 3 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 3$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 0$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 1$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 2$
Explanation. The maximum of two linear functions is convex. $\max\{x, 2-x\} \ge 1$ because the two terms sum to $2$, with equality only for $x = 2 - x$, that is, $x = 1$. At $x = 2$ it equals $\max\{2, 0\} = 2$, not $0$.
co-f-363 · level facile · notes
In the convex minimization problem $\min\{\, x^2 \ \text{subject to}\ 1 \le x \le 3 \,\}$, what are the local minimum points of $x^2$ in $[1, 3]$?
  1. none, because the derivative does not vanish on $[1,3]$
  2. only $x = 1$, which is also the optimal solution
  3. $x = 0$, where the derivative vanishes
  4. $x = 1$ and $x = 3$, the endpoints
Explanation. $x^2$ increases on $[1,3]$: near $1$ we have $x^2 \ge 1$, while near $3$ there are points with value less than $9$. The point $x = 0$ is not feasible; a local minimum does not require a zero derivative.
co-f-372 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is called convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. What can be said of the problem $\max\{\, -x_1^2 - x_2^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 \ge 1 \,\}$?
  1. it is concave: concave objective, convex half-plane
  2. it is convex, because the feasible region is convex
  3. it is neither convex nor concave, because the region is unbounded
  4. it is convex, because it is in maximization form
Explanation. $-x_1^2 - x_2^2$ is the opposite of a convex function, hence concave; $x_1 + x_2 \ge 1$ is a half-plane, which is convex. The form and boundedness do not matter.
co-f-373 · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is called convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. What can be said of the problem $\min\{\, x_1^2 - x_2^2 \ \text{subject to}\ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 \,\}$?
  1. it is convex, because $\R^2$ is convex
  2. it is concave, because it contains a negative term
  3. it is convex, because it is a sum of squares
  4. neither: $f$ is neither convex nor concave
Explanation. Along the $x_1$ axis the function is $x_1^2$ (convex), along the $x_2$ axis it is $-x_2^2$ (concave): the inequality fails in one direction or the other. ${\boldsymbol Q} = \mathrm{diag}(1, -1)$ is not positive semidefinite, and neither is $-{\boldsymbol Q}$.
co-f-374 · level facile · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the concave maximization problem $P$: $\max\{\, 5 - (x_1-1)^2 - (x_2-2)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 \le 3 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{3}{2} \\ \frac{3}{2} \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = \frac{9}{2}$
Explanation. $5 - (x_1-1)^2 - (x_2-2)^2 \le 5$ everywhere, with equality at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, which is feasible ($1 + 2 = 3 \le 3$, on the boundary): it is the optimal solution.
co-f-375 · level facile · notes
In the examples of chapter 6 one first checks that the feasible region is convex and the objective function convex (or concave), then looks for a local minimum (or maximum). Why this order?
  1. to compute the optimal value without finding the optimal solution
  2. because only then is a local optimum automatically an optimal solution
  3. because the derivative exists only for convex functions
  4. because without convexity there are no local minima
Explanation. The proposition and the corollary of the chapter turn a local optimum into a global one only in convex minimization and concave maximization problems; elsewhere local minima exist but may not be optimal.
co-f-376 · level facile · notes
In the convex minimization problem $\min\{\, |x_1| + |x_2| \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 = 2 \,\}$, the point $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ has value $2$ and is a local minimum. What are the optimal solutions?
  1. every ${\boldsymbol x} \ge {\boldsymbol 0}$ with $x_1 + x_2 = 2$, value $2$
  2. only $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, because a convex problem has a single optimum
  3. only $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. none: the problem is unbounded below
Explanation. On $x_1 + x_2 = 2$ we have $|x_1| + |x_2| \ge x_1 + x_2 = 2$, with equality when $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$: infinitely many optimal points. The proposition guarantees that every local minimum is optimal, not that the optimum is unique.

Generated questions (47)

Each generator builds a new instance, with different numbers, at every start: a few examples here.

1. Convex sets (5)

Example 1 · co-g-cc2 · level facile · notes
Let ${\boldsymbol p} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol w} = \begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 7 \end{pmatrix}$. What is their convex combination $\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}$ with scalar $\lambda = \frac{1}{4}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{21}{4} \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{15}{4} \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{9}{2} \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{9}{4} \\ \frac{5}{2} \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. Component by component: $\frac{1}{4}\cdot 3 + \frac{3}{4}\cdot 6 = \frac{21}{4}$ and $\frac{1}{4}\cdot 3 + \frac{3}{4}\cdot 7 = 6$. Typical mistake: swapping $\lambda$ and $1-\lambda$.
Example 2 · co-g-cc2 · level facile · notes
Let ${\boldsymbol p} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 7 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol w} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$. What is their convex combination $\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w}$ with scalar $\lambda = \frac{3}{4}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{3}{4} \\ \frac{19}{4} \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{4} \\ \frac{1}{4} \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{2} \\ \frac{5}{2} \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{3}{4} \\ \frac{15}{4} \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. Component by component: $\frac{3}{4}\cdot 1 + \frac{1}{4}\cdot 0 = \frac{3}{4}$ and $\frac{3}{4}\cdot 7 + \frac{1}{4}\cdot -2 = \frac{19}{4}$. Typical mistake: swapping $\lambda$ and $1-\lambda$.
Example 1 · co-g-cc3 · level facile · notes
What is the convex combination $\lambda_1 {\boldsymbol p}^{(1)} + \lambda_2 {\boldsymbol p}^{(2)} + \lambda_3 {\boldsymbol p}^{(3)}$ of the points ${\boldsymbol p}^{(1)} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol p}^{(2)} = \begin{pmatrix} 7 \\ 8 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol p}^{(3)} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 7 \end{pmatrix}$, with scalars $\lambda_1 = \frac{1}{2}$, $\lambda_2 = \frac{1}{3}$, $\lambda_3 = \frac{1}{6}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{25}{6} \\ \frac{35}{6} \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ \frac{37}{6} \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{23}{6} \\ 7 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{10}{3} \\ \frac{19}{3} \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The point is $\sum_{k=1}^{3} \lambda_k {\boldsymbol p}^{(k)} = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{25}{6} \\ \frac{35}{6} \end{pmatrix}$: each scalar multiplies <em>its own</em> point. The other options pair the scalars with the wrong points or use the centroid (all scalars equal to $\frac{1}{3}$).
Example 2 · co-g-cc3 · level facile · notes
