Example 1 · co-g-ball · level facile · notes
Which point belongs to the convex set $\{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}\|_{2} \le 4 \}$, that is, to the ball of the norm $2$ with center $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $4$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. For the correct point ${\boldsymbol x}$, the difference from the center is ${\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix}$ and $(0)^2 + (-4)^2 = 16 \le 16$. The other points exceed the radius in the norm $2$ (but would be inside with another norm: typical mistake).
Example 2 · co-g-ball · level facile · notes
Which point belongs to the convex set $\{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}\|_{2} \le 2 \}$, that is, to the ball of the norm $2$ with center $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $2$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. For the correct point ${\boldsymbol x}$, the difference from the center is ${\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $(1)^2 + (1)^2 = 2 \le 4$. The other points exceed the radius in the norm $2$ (but would be inside with another norm: typical mistake).
Example 1 · co-g-ballcap · level media · notes
Let $A = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}\|_1 \le 4 \}$ (ball of the norm $1$) and $B = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}\|_\infty \le 3 \}$ (ball of the norm $\infty$). Which point belongs to the convex set $A \cap B$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. For $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$: distance in the norm $1$ from $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ equal to $1 \le 4$ and distance in the norm $\infty$ from $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ equal to $2 \le 3$: it belongs to both sets. Each of the other points belongs to only one of the two (typical mistake: confusing intersection and union).
Example 2 · co-g-ballcap · level media · notes
Let $A = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}\|_1 \le 2 \}$ (ball of the norm $1$) and $B = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}\|_\infty \le 3 \}$ (ball of the norm $\infty$). Which point belongs to the convex set $A \cap B$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. For $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$: distance in the norm $1$ from $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ equal to $2 \le 2$ and distance in the norm $\infty$ from $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ equal to $3 \le 3$: it belongs to both sets. Each of the other points belongs to only one of the two (typical mistake: confusing intersection and union).
Example 1 · co-g-bary · level complessa · notes
Let ${\boldsymbol p}^{(1)} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol p}^{(2)} = \begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol p}^{(3)} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$. The point ${\boldsymbol q} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ can be written in exactly one way as $\lambda_1 {\boldsymbol p}^{(1)} + \lambda_2 {\boldsymbol p}^{(2)} + \lambda_3 {\boldsymbol p}^{(3)}$ with $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \lambda_3 = 1$. What are the scalars, and is ${\boldsymbol q}$ a convex combination of the three points?
  1. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{4})$: it is a convex combination
  2. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{4})$: it is not a convex combination
  3. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{4})$: it is a convex combination
  4. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{2})$: it is a convex combination
Explanation. The two coordinate equations and $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \lambda_3 = 1$ give $(\frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{4})$. A convex combination requires nonnegative scalars summing to $1$: here they are all $\ge 0$, and ${\boldsymbol q}$ lies in the triangle.
Example 2 · co-g-bary · level complessa · notes
Let ${\boldsymbol p}^{(1)} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol p}^{(2)} = \begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol p}^{(3)} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 7 \end{pmatrix}$. The point ${\boldsymbol q} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$ can be written in exactly one way as $\lambda_1 {\boldsymbol p}^{(1)} + \lambda_2 {\boldsymbol p}^{(2)} + \lambda_3 {\boldsymbol p}^{(3)}$ with $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \lambda_3 = 1$. What are the scalars, and is ${\boldsymbol q}$ a convex combination of the three points?
  1. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{1}{2}, \frac{3}{4}, -\frac{1}{4})$: it is not a convex combination
  2. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{1}{2}, \frac{3}{4}, -\frac{1}{4})$: it is a convex combination
  3. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{3}{4}, -\frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{2})$: it is not a convex combination
  4. $(\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) = (\frac{1}{2}, -\frac{1}{4}, \frac{3}{4})$: it is not a convex combination
Explanation. The two coordinate equations and $\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \lambda_3 = 1$ give $(\frac{1}{2}, \frac{3}{4}, -\frac{1}{4})$. A convex combination requires nonnegative scalars summing to $1$: here one scalar is negative, and ${\boldsymbol q}$ lies outside the triangle.

2. Functions of several variables (5)

Example 1 · co-g-grad · level facile · notes
Given $f: \R^2 \to \R$, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 x_2 + x_2^2 + 4x_1$, what is the gradient $\nabla f$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ -8 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ 7 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = \begin{pmatrix} 2x_1 x_2 + 4 \\ x_1^2 + 2x_2 \end{pmatrix}$, which at $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$ equals $\begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$. Typical mistakes: forgetting the factor $2$ of the powers or swapping the components.
Example 2 · co-g-grad · level facile · notes
Given $f: \R^2 \to \R$, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = -x_1^2 x_2 + 3x_2^2 - 2x_1$, what is the gradient $\nabla f$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ -10 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -10 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -10 \\ -6 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ -7 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla f({\boldsymbol x}) = \begin{pmatrix} -2x_1 x_2 - 2 \\ -x_1^2 + 6x_2 \end{pmatrix}$, which at $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ equals $\begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ -10 \end{pmatrix}$. Typical mistakes: forgetting the factor $2$ of the powers or swapping the components.
Example 1 · co-g-hess · level facile · notes
Given $f: \R^2 \to \R$, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 x_2 + x_1 x_2 + 2x_2^3$, what is the Hessian $\nabla^2 f$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 & 7 \\ 7 & -24 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 & 4 \\ 4 & -24 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 & 7 \\ 7 & 24 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -24 & 7 \\ 7 & -4 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla^2 f({\boldsymbol x}) = \begin{pmatrix} 2x_2 & 2x_1 + 1 \\ 2x_1 + 1 & 12x_2 \end{pmatrix}$, which at $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$ equals $\begin{pmatrix} -4 & 7 \\ 7 & -24 \end{pmatrix}$ (symmetric). Typical mistakes: factor $2$ forgotten in the mixed derivative, first derivative instead of the second, swapped diagonal.
Example 2 · co-g-hess · level facile · notes
