Typical mistakes: factor $2$ forgotten in the mixed derivative, first derivative instead of the second, swapped diagonal.
Example 1 · co-g-level · level facile · notes
For $f: \R^2 \to \R$, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = (x_1 - 1)^2 + (x_2 - 1)^2 + 4$, what is the level set $L(-5) = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = -5 \}$?
  1. the empty set
  2. the single point $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. the circle with center $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $3$
  4. the circle with center $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $3$
Explanation. $L(-5)$: we would need $(x_1 - 1)^2 + (x_2 - 1)^2 = -9 < 0$, impossible: the set is empty.
Example 2 · co-g-level · level facile · notes
For $f: \R^2 \to \R$, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = (x_1 - 3)^2 + (x_2 - 2)^2 + 5$, what is the level set $L(5) = \{ {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = 5 \}$?
  1. the single point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. the empty set
  3. the single point $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. the circle with center $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $5$
Explanation. $L(5)$: $(x_1 - 3)^2 + (x_2 - 2)^2 = 0$, a vanishing sum of squares: only the point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$.
Example 1 · co-g-descent · level media · notes
Given $f: \R^2 \to \R$, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2(x_1 - 3)^2 + 2(x_2 + 3)^2$, starting from $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$ we move by a step $\alpha = \frac{1}{2}$ along the direction of steepest descent, that is, to the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} - \alpha\, \nabla f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$. What is the new point?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ -6 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, hence $\bar{\boldsymbol x} - \frac{1}{2}\,\nabla f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix}$. Typical mistakes: the sign $+$ (direction of steepest ascent), the step $\alpha$ forgotten, the factor $2$ of the gradient forgotten.
Example 2 · co-g-descent · level media · notes
Given $f: \R^2 \to \R$, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = (x_1 + 1)^2 + 3(x_2 + 3)^2$, starting from $\bar{\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ we move by a step $\alpha = \frac{1}{3}$ along the direction of steepest descent, that is, to the point $\bar{\boldsymbol x} - \alpha\, \nabla f(\bar{\boldsymbol x})$. What is the new point?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -\frac{2}{3} \\ -9 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{2}{3} \\ 15 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -33 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -\frac{1}{3} \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 36 \end{pmatrix}$, hence $\bar{\boldsymbol x} - \frac{1}{3}\,\nabla f(\bar{\boldsymbol x}) = \begin{pmatrix} -\frac{2}{3} \\ -9 \end{pmatrix}$. Typical mistakes: the sign $+$ (direction of steepest ascent), the step $\alpha$ forgotten, the factor $2$ of the gradient forgotten.
Example 1 · co-g-tangent · level media · notes
For $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + 3x_2^2$, the point $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ lies on the level set $\{\, {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = 4 \,\}$. Which direction is tangent to the level set at that point?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The gradient $\nabla f = \begin{pmatrix} 2x_1 \\ 6x_2 \end{pmatrix}$ equals $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$, parallel to $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, and is perpendicular to the level set: the tangent is perpendicular to the gradient, that is, $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ (zero dot product). The other directions have a nonzero dot product with the gradient.
Example 2 · co-g-tangent · level media · notes
For $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2x_1^2 + 3x_2^2$, the point $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$ lies on the level set $\{\, {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : f({\boldsymbol x}) = 14 \,\}$. Which direction is tangent to the level set at that point?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The gradient $\nabla f = \begin{pmatrix} 4x_1 \\ 6x_2 \end{pmatrix}$ equals $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -12 \end{pmatrix}$, parallel to $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$, and is perpendicular to the level set: the tangent is perpendicular to the gradient, that is, $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ (zero dot product). The other directions have a nonzero dot product with the gradient.

3. Convex and concave functions (5)

Example 1 · co-g-norms · level facile · notes
For the vector ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} -9 \\ 6 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, what are the norms $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2$ and $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty$?
  1. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 17$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 11$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 9$
  2. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = -1$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 11$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 9$
  3. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 17$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 121$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 9$
  4. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 17$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 11$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 2$
Explanation. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 9 + 6 + 2 = 17$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = \sqrt{81 + 36 + 4} = 11$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = \max\{9, 6, 2\} = 9$. Typical mistakes: absolute values forgotten, square root forgotten, minimum instead of maximum.
Example 2 · co-g-norms · level facile · notes
For the vector ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -2 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$, what are the norms $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2$ and $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty$?
  1. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 5$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 3$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 2$
  2. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = -3$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 3$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 2$
  3. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 5$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 9$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 2$
  4. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 5$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = 3$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = 1$
Explanation. $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_1 = 1 + 2 + 2 = 5$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_2 = \sqrt{1 + 4 + 4} = 3$, $\|{\boldsymbol x}\|_\infty = \max\{1, 2, 2\} = 2$. Typical mistakes: absolute values forgotten, square root forgotten, minimum instead of maximum.
Example 1 · co-g-chord · level facile · notes
For the convex function $f(x) = (x + 2)^2$, with $p = 2$, $w = -3$ and $\lambda = \frac{3}{4}$, what are the two sides $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w)$ and $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w)$ of the convexity inequality?
  1. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{121}{16}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{49}{4}$
  2. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{49}{4}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{121}{16}$
  3. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{121}{16}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{17}{2}$
  4. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{121}{16}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{47}{4}$
Explanation. $\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w = \frac{3}{4}$ and $f(\frac{3}{4}) = \frac{121}{16}$; $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{3}{4}\cdot 16 + \frac{1}{4}\cdot 1 = \frac{49}{4}$. As it must be, $\frac{121}{16} \le \frac{49}{4}$.
Example 2 · co-g-chord · level facile · notes
For the convex function $f(x) = (x - 1)^2$, with $p = 2$, $w = 5$ and $\lambda = \frac{1}{4}$, what are the two sides $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w)$ and $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w)$ of the convexity inequality?
  1. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{169}{16}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{49}{4}$
  2. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{49}{4}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{169}{16}$
  3. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{169}{16}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{17}{2}$
  4. $f(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w) = \frac{169}{16}$, $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{13}{4}$
Explanation. $\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w = \frac{17}{4}$ and $f(\frac{17}{4}) = \frac{169}{16}$; $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w) = \frac{1}{4}\cdot 1 + \frac{3}{4}\cdot 16 = \frac{49}{4}$. As it must be, $\frac{169}{16} \le \frac{49}{4}$.
Example 1 · co-g-normchord · level media · notes
For the convex function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}\|_{\infty} + 3$ in $\R^2$, with ${\boldsymbol p} = \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol w} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\lambda = \frac{3}{4}$, what are (in this order) the two sides $f(\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w})$ and $\lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ of the convexity inequality?
  1. $\frac{23}{4}$ and $\frac{25}{4}$
  2. $\frac{25}{4}$ and $\frac{23}{4}$
  3. $\frac{11}{4}$ and $\frac{25}{4}$
  4. $\frac{23}{4}$ and $\frac{19}{4}$
Explanation. $\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w} = \begin{pmatrix} -\frac{7}{4} \\ \frac{7}{2} \end{pmatrix}$ and $f(\begin{pmatrix} -\frac{7}{4} \\ \frac{7}{2} \end{pmatrix}) = \frac{23}{4}$; $f({\boldsymbol p}) = 7$, $f({\boldsymbol w}) = 4$ and $\frac{3}{4}\cdot 7 + \frac{1}{4}\cdot 4 = \frac{25}{4}$. As it must be, $\frac{23}{4} \le \frac{25}{4}$. Typical mistakes: forgetting the constant $3$, swapping the weights $\lambda$ and $1-\lambda$.
Example 2 · co-g-normchord · level media · notes
For the convex function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = \|{\boldsymbol x} - \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}\|_{1} - 2$ in $\R^2$, with ${\boldsymbol p} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol w} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\lambda = \frac{3}{4}$, what are (in this order) the two sides $f(\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w})$ and $\lambda f({\boldsymbol p}) + (1-\lambda) f({\boldsymbol w})$ of the convexity inequality?
  1. $\frac{9}{2}$ and $5$
  2. $5$ and $\frac{9}{2}$
  3. $\frac{13}{2}$ and $5$
  4. $\frac{9}{2}$ and $3$
Explanation. $\lambda {\boldsymbol p} + (1-\lambda){\boldsymbol w} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ \frac{7}{2} \end{pmatrix}$ and $f(\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ \frac{7}{2} \end{pmatrix}) = \frac{9}{2}$; $f({\boldsymbol p}) = 6$, $f({\boldsymbol w}) = 2$ and $\frac{3}{4}\cdot 6 + \frac{1}{4}\cdot 2 = 5$. As it must be, $\frac{9}{2} \le 5$. Typical mistakes: forgetting the constant $-2$, swapping the weights $\lambda$ and $1-\lambda$.
Example 1 · co-g-levelvert · level complessa · notes
The set $\{\, {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \max\{|x_1|, |x_2|\} - 3 \le 0,~ -x_1 - x_2 - 2 \le 0 \,\}$ is convex, because it is defined by constraints $g_i({\boldsymbol x}) \le 0$ with convex functions $g_i$ (a norm and a linear function). It is a polygon: what are its vertices?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The ball of norm $\infty$ is the square with vertices $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$; the line $-x_1 - x_2 = 2$ cuts off the vertex $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$ and meets two sides at two new vertices. The vertices are $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$.
Example 2 · co-g-levelvert · level complessa · notes
The set $\{\, {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : \max\{|x_1|, |x_2|\} - 4 \le 0,~ -x_1 - x_2 - 2 \le 0 \,\}$ is convex, because it is defined by constraints $g_i({\boldsymbol x}) \le 0$ with convex functions $g_i$ (a norm and a linear function). It is a polygon: what are its vertices?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The ball of norm $\infty$ is the square with vertices $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$; the line $-x_1 - x_2 = 2$ cuts off the vertex $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix}$ and meets two sides at two new vertices. The vertices are $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix},\ \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$.
Example 1 · co-g-cubicchord · level media · notes
For $f(x) = x^3 + 2x$, with $p = -2$, $w = 2$ and $\lambda = \frac{3}{4}$, what are $f\big(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w\big)$ and $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w)$, and what can be concluded?
  1. $-3$ and $-6$: $f$ is not convex on $[p, w]$
  2. $-3$ and $-6$: $f$ is concave on $[p, w]$
  3. $-3$ and $-6$: $f$ is not concave on $[p, w]$
  4. $3$ and $6$: $f$ is not concave on $[p, w]$
Explanation. $\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w = -1$, $f(-1) = -3$, while $\frac{3}{4} \cdot (-12) + \frac{1}{4} \cdot 12 = -6$. The point of the graph lies above the chord: the convexity inequality is violated. A single pair of points can rule out a property, not prove it.
Example 2 · co-g-cubicchord · level media · notes
For $f(x) = x^3 + 2x$, with $p = -2$, $w = 3$ and $\lambda = \frac{1}{4}$, what are $f\big(\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w\big)$ and $\lambda f(p) + (1-\lambda) f(w)$, and what can be concluded?
  1. $\frac{567}{64}$ and $\frac{87}{4}$: $f$ is not concave on $[p, w]$
  2. $\frac{567}{64}$ and $\frac{87}{4}$: $f$ is convex on $[p, w]$
  3. $\frac{567}{64}$ and $\frac{87}{4}$: $f$ is not convex on $[p, w]$
  4. $-\frac{123}{64}$ and $-\frac{3}{4}$: $f$ is not concave on $[p, w]$
Explanation. $\lambda p + (1-\lambda) w = \frac{7}{4}$, $f(\frac{7}{4}) = \frac{567}{64}$, while $\frac{1}{4} \cdot (-12) + \frac{3}{4} \cdot 33 = \frac{87}{4}$. The point of the graph lies below the chord: the concavity inequality is violated. A single pair of points can rule out a property, not prove it.

4. Quadratic functions (5)

Example 1 · co-g-sym · level facile · notes
The non-symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & -6 \\ 2 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$ defines the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ of ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$. Which symmetric matrix defines the same function?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -2 \\ -2 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -6 \\ -6 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -4 \\ -4 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. Each off-diagonal pair is replaced by its average: $\frac{-6 + (2)}{2} = -2$, that is, we use $\tfrac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol Q} + {\boldsymbol Q}')$. With $-4$ off the diagonal the coefficient of $x_1 x_2$ would double.
Example 2 · co-g-sym · level facile · notes
The non-symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 4 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$ defines the quadratic function $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$ of ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$. Which symmetric matrix defines the same function?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 3 \\ 3 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 4 \\ 4 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 2 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 6 \\ 6 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. Each off-diagonal pair is replaced by its average: $\frac{4 + (2)}{2} = 3$, that is, we use $\tfrac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol Q} + {\boldsymbol Q}')$. With $6$ off the diagonal the coefficient of $x_1 x_2$ would double.
Example 1 · co-g-quadgrad · level facile · notes
Let $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}' {\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol c} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix}$. What is $\nabla g$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 12 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 8 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 8 \\ 10 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 14 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla g({\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}$, with ${\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$: $2\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -4 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 12 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$. Typical mistakes: factor $2$ forgotten, ${\boldsymbol c}$ forgotten or subtracted.
Example 2 · co-g-quadgrad · level facile · notes
Let $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}' {\boldsymbol x}$ with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1 \\ -1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$ and ${\boldsymbol c} = \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$. What is $\nabla g$ at the point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 7 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. $\nabla g({\boldsymbol x}) = 2\,{\boldsymbol Q}\,{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}$, with ${\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$: $2\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$. Typical mistakes: factor $2$ forgotten, ${\boldsymbol c}$ forgotten or subtracted.
Example 1 · co-g-psd · level media · notes
Which of the following quadratic functions $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$, with $\,{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$, is convex in $\R^2$?
  1. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 3x_1^2 + 2x_1 x_2 + 3x_2^2$
  2. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + 6x_1 x_2 + 3x_2^2$
  3. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2x_1^2 + 6x_1 x_2 + x_2^2$
  4. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 3x_1^2 - 2x_1 x_2 - x_2^2$
Explanation. The correct function can be written as $3\left(x_1 + \frac{1}{3}\, x_2\right)^2 + \frac{8}{3}\, x_2^2 \ge 0$: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite and hence the function is convex (a quadratic function is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite). The others do not have ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite: for $x_1^2 + 6x_1 x_2 + 3x_2^2$ the vector $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $-6 < 0$; for $2x_1^2 + 6x_1 x_2 + x_2^2$ the vector $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $-14 < 0$; for $3x_1^2 - 2x_1 x_2 - x_2^2$ the vector $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $-1 < 0$. A positive diagonal is not enough.
Example 2 · co-g-psd · level media · notes
Which of the following quadratic functions $f({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x}$, with $\,{\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$, is convex in $\R^2$?
  1. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 4x_1^2 + 2x_1 x_2 + x_2^2$
  2. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2x_1^2 - 6x_1 x_2 + 2x_2^2$
  3. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = x_1^2 + 4x_1 x_2 + x_2^2$
  4. $f({\boldsymbol x}) = -3x_1^2 - 2x_1 x_2 + 4x_2^2$
Explanation. The correct function can be written as $4\left(x_1 + \frac{1}{4}\, x_2\right)^2 + \frac{3}{4}\, x_2^2 \ge 0$: ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite and hence the function is convex (a quadratic function is convex if and only if ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite). The others do not have ${\boldsymbol Q}$ positive semidefinite: for $2x_1^2 - 6x_1 x_2 + 2x_2^2$ the vector $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $-10 < 0$; for $x_1^2 + 4x_1 x_2 + x_2^2$ the vector $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $-3 < 0$; for $-3x_1^2 - 2x_1 x_2 + 4x_2^2$ the vector $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ gives $-3 < 0$. A positive diagonal is not enough.
Example 1 · co-g-stationary · level complessa · notes
Let $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}' {\boldsymbol x} + d = 3x_1^2 + 4x_1 x_2 - 2x_2^2 - 10x_1 + 5$, with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 2 \\ 2 & -2 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol c} = \begin{pmatrix} -10 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $d = 5$. Which statement on convexity and on the point where the gradient vanishes is correct?
  1. $g$ is not convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 0$
  2. $g$ is convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 0$
  3. $g$ is not convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 5$
  4. $g$ is not convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 20$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not positive semidefinite (it has a negative diagonal entry), hence $g$ is not convex. $\nabla g({\boldsymbol x}) = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ gives ${\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, that is, ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, with $g = 0$. Typical mistakes: factor $2$ forgotten ($\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$), wrong sign of ${\boldsymbol c}$ ($\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$).
Example 2 · co-g-stationary · level complessa · notes
Let $g({\boldsymbol x}) = {\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c}' {\boldsymbol x} + d = -2x_1 x_2 + 3x_2^2 - 2x_1 + 2x_2 + 5$, with ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1 \\ -1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$, ${\boldsymbol c} = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ and $d = 5$. Which statement on convexity and on the point where the gradient vanishes is correct?
  1. $g$ is not convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 6$
  2. $g$ is convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 6$
  3. $g$ is not convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 5$
  4. $g$ is not convex; $\nabla g$ vanishes at $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, where $g = 2$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is not positive semidefinite ($\det {\boldsymbol Q} = -1 < 0$), hence $g$ is not convex. $\nabla g({\boldsymbol x}) = 2{\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} + {\boldsymbol c} = {\boldsymbol 0}$ gives ${\boldsymbol Q}{\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, that is, ${\boldsymbol x} = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, with $g = 6$. Typical mistakes: factor $2$ forgotten ($\begin{pmatrix} -4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$), wrong sign of ${\boldsymbol c}$ ($\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$).
Example 1 · co-g-witness · level media · notes
A symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite if ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$. For ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 2 \\ 2 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$, which vector shows that ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is <em>not</em> positive semidefinite?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = 3x_1^2 + 4x_1 x_2$: at the four vectors it equals $-5$, $3$, $4$, $0$. One vector with a negative value is enough, $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$; a nonnegative value at some vectors proves nothing.
Example 2 · co-g-witness · level media · notes
A symmetric matrix ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is positive semidefinite if ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} \ge 0$ for every ${\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2$. For ${\boldsymbol Q} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & -2 \\ -2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}$, which vector shows that ${\boldsymbol Q}$ is <em>not</em> positive semidefinite?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. ${\boldsymbol x}' {\boldsymbol Q}\, {\boldsymbol x} = x_1^2 - 4x_1 x_2 + 3x_2^2$: at the four vectors it equals $-1$, $21$, $5$, $0$. One vector with a negative value is enough, $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$; a nonnegative value at some vectors proves nothing.

5. Optimization problems (14)

Example 1 · co-g-bounds · level facile · notes
For the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x - 1)^2 + 1 \ \text{subject to}\ -3 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ the feasible solutions $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} = (4)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)} = (2)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(3)} = (-3)$ are known. What is the best bound on the optimal value $z(P)$ that they provide?
  1. $z(P) \le 2$
  2. $z(P) \le 17$
  3. $2 \le z(P)$
  4. $17 \le z(P)$
Explanation. The values are $10$, $2$, $17$. In minimization form every feasible solution gives an upper bound and the best one is the smallest.
Example 2 · co-g-bounds · level facile · notes
For the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x - 3)^2 + 3 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 7 \,\}$ the feasible solutions $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} = (4)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)} = (3)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(3)} = (-1)$ are known. What is the best bound on the optimal value $z(P)$ that they provide?
  1. $z(P) \le 3$
  2. $z(P) \le 19$
  3. $3 \le z(P)$
  4. $19 \le z(P)$
Explanation. The values are $4$, $3$, $19$. In minimization form every feasible solution gives an upper bound and the best one is the smallest.
Example 1 · co-g-props · level facile · notes
Over a feasible region $F$ the objective function $f$ has minimum $0$ and maximum $5$ (both attained). What is the optimal value $\min\{\, 3\, f({\boldsymbol x}) + 5 \ \text{subject to}\ {\boldsymbol x} \in F \,\}$?
  1. $5$
  2. $20$
  3. $0$
  4. $-5$
Explanation. The constant is added outside and, with $a = 3 > 0$, $\min\{a f\} = a \min\{f\}$: $3\cdot(0) + 5 = 5$.
Example 2 · co-g-props · level facile · notes
Over a feasible region $F$ the objective function $f$ has minimum $-1$ and maximum $6$ (both attained). What is the optimal value $\min\{\, -2\, f({\boldsymbol x}) - 5 \ \text{subject to}\ {\boldsymbol x} \in F \,\}$?
  1. $-17$
  2. $-3$
  3. $-12$
  4. $17$
Explanation. The constant is added outside and, with $a = -2 < 0$, $\min\{a f\} = a \max\{f\}$: $-2\cdot 6 - 5 = -17$. Typical mistake: using the minimum of $f$.
Example 1 · co-g-neigh · level facile · notes
Which point belongs to the spherical neighborhood $N\left(\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}, 5\right)$ with center $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $5$, that is, the set of the points at Euclidean distance <em>less</em> than $5$ from the center?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 7 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The correct point has squared distance $17 < 25$ from the center. The point $\begin{pmatrix} 7 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$ has distance exactly $5$: it lies on the circle, which does not belong to the neighborhood (strict inequality). The others have distance greater than $5$.
Example 2 · co-g-neigh · level facile · notes
Which point belongs to the spherical neighborhood $N\left(\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}, 2\right)$ with center $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $2$, that is, the set of the points at Euclidean distance <em>less</em> than $2$ from the center?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. The correct point has squared distance $1 < 4$ from the center. The point $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$ has distance exactly $2$: it lies on the circle, which does not belong to the neighborhood (strict inequality). The others have distance greater than $2$.
Example 1 · co-g-interval · level media · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x - 1)^2 + 1 \ \text{subject to}\ 2 \le x \le 8 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 2$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (8)$, $z(P) = 50$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 1$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 10$
Explanation. The point $1$ is not feasible; over the feasible region $(x-1)^2$ is smallest at the endpoint closest to $1$, that is, $2$, with $z(P) = 2$.
Example 2 · co-g-interval · level media · notes
What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, (x - 3)^2 + 3 \ \text{subject to}\ 1 \le x \le 7 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (7)$, $z(P) = 19$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 7$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = 3$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (7)$, $z(P) = 103$
Explanation. In maximization form the endpoint farthest from $3$ matters: $f(1) = 7$ and $f(7) = 19$, hence $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (7)$ and $z(P) = 19$.
Example 1 · co-g-classify · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\max\{\, -(x - 1)^2 + 3 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in [1, 5] \,\}$, with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution (if any) and $z(P)$ the optimal value. Which statement on $P$ is correct?
  1. it has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, with $z(P) = 3$
  2. it has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, with $z(P) = -13$
  3. it has an unattainable supremum $3$: there is no optimal solution
  4. it is unbounded, with $z(P) = +\infty$
Explanation. The parabola has vertex at $x = 1$ (value $3$) and opens downward: in maximization form what matters is the point of $F$ closest to the vertex. Here the best point of the closure of $F$ is $x = 1$, which belongs to $F$: it is the optimal solution, with $z(P) = 3$. Typical mistakes: ignoring whether the endpoints belong to $F$, or using the vertex in the wrong form.
Example 2 · co-g-classify · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x - 3)^2 - 1 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in [2, 7) \,\}$, with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution (if any) and $z(P)$ the optimal value. Which statement on $P$ is correct?
  1. it has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, with $z(P) = -1$
  2. it has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, with $z(P) = 0$
  3. it has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (7)$, with $z(P) = 15$
  4. it has an unattainable infimum $0$: there is no optimal solution
Explanation. The parabola has vertex at $x = 3$ (value $-1$) and opens upward: in minimization form what matters is the point of $F$ closest to the vertex. Here the best point of the closure of $F$ is $x = 3$, which belongs to $F$: it is the optimal solution, with $z(P) = -1$. Typical mistakes: ignoring whether the endpoints belong to $F$, or using the vertex in the wrong form.
Example 1 · co-g-feas · level facile · notes
An optimization problem has feasible region $F = \{\, {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1 + 2x_2 \le 1,~ x_2 \ge 0,~ (x_1 - 1)^2 + (x_2 - 2)^2 \le 9 \,\}$. Which point is a feasible solution, that is, a point of $F$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. A point is feasible if it satisfies <em>all</em> the constraints: $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ satisfies them; $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$ violates the first constraint, $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ violates the second constraint and $\begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ violates the third constraint.
Example 2 · co-g-feas · level facile · notes
An optimization problem has feasible region $F = \{\, {\boldsymbol x} \in \R^2 : x_1 + x_2 \le 2,~ x_2 \ge 0,~ (x_1 - 2)^2 + x_2^2 \le 4 \,\}$. Which point is a feasible solution, that is, a point of $F$?
  1. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  2. $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
  3. $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$
  4. $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$
Explanation. A point is feasible if it satisfies <em>all</em> the constraints: $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ satisfies them; $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ violates the first constraint, $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ violates the second constraint and $\begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ violates the third constraint.
Example 1 · co-g-status · level facile · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x + 1 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in F \,\}$, with feasible region $F = \{\, x \in \R : x \ge 1,~ x \le -3 \,\}$, optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ (if any) and optimal value $z(P)$. Which statement is correct?
  1. it is infeasible: $F = \emptyset$ and no optimal solution
  2. it has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-3)$ and optimal value $z(P) = -2$
  3. it has an unattainable supremum $2$: no optimal solution
  4. it is unbounded: $z(P) = +\infty$ and no optimal solution
Explanation. The objective function is linear and increasing; in maximization form it pays to move to the right. Here no $x$ satisfies both $x \ge 1$ and $x \le -3$: $F$ is empty.
Example 2 · co-g-status · level facile · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -x + 3 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in F \,\}$, with feasible region $F = [-1, 3]$, optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ (if any) and optimal value $z(P)$. Which statement is correct?
  1. it has optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$ and optimal value $z(P) = 0$
  2. it has an unattainable infimum $0$: no optimal solution
  3. it is unbounded: $z(P) = -\infty$ and no optimal solution
  4. it is infeasible: $F = \emptyset$ and no optimal solution
Explanation. The objective function is linear and decreasing; in minimization form it pays to move to the right. Here the endpoint $3$ matters, and it belongs to $F$.
Example 1 · co-g-finite · level facile · notes
The problem $P$: $\max\{\, x_1 + x_2 + 2 \ \text{subject to}\ {\boldsymbol x} \in F \,\}$ has the finite feasible region $F = \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix} \right\}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 6$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 3$
Explanation. We compare the value of the objective function at every feasible solution: $3$, $6$, $4$, $5$. In maximization form the largest wins, $6$, attained at $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$.
Example 2 · co-g-finite · level facile · notes
The problem $P$: $\min\{\, -x_1 + 3x_2 - 1 \ \text{subject to}\ {\boldsymbol x} \in F \,\}$ has the finite feasible region $F = \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}, \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix} \right\}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 2$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 9$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 11$
Explanation. We compare the value of the objective function at every feasible solution: $11$, $2$, $4$, $9$. In minimization form the smallest wins, $2$, attained at $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$.
Example 1 · co-g-neighcap · level facile · notes
The definition of a local minimum point uses the spherical neighborhood $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$, the set of the points at distance less than $\delta$ from $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$. In $\R$, with feasible region $F = [0, 5]$, $\bar x = 5$ and $\delta = 3$, which set is $N\big((5), 3\big) \cap F$?
  1. $(2, 5]$
  2. $[2, 5)$
  3. $(2, 8)$
  4. $[2, 8]$
Explanation. In $\R$ the neighborhood is the open interval $(2, 8)$; intersecting it with $F = [0, 5]$ gives $(2, 5]$: an endpoint of $F$ falling inside the neighborhood stays included, an endpoint of the neighborhood stays excluded.
Example 2 · co-g-neighcap · level facile · notes
The definition of a local minimum point uses the spherical neighborhood $N(\bar{\boldsymbol x}, \delta)$, the set of the points at distance less than $\delta$ from $\bar{\boldsymbol x}$. In $\R$, with feasible region $F = [-1, 6]$, $\bar x = -1$ and $\delta = 1$, which set is $N\big((-1), 1\big) \cap F$?
  1. $[-1, 0)$
  2. $(-1, 0]$
  3. $(-2, 0)$
  4. $[-2, 0]$
Explanation. In $\R$ the neighborhood is the open interval $(-2, 0)$; intersecting it with $F = [-1, 6]$ gives $[-1, 0)$: an endpoint of $F$ falling inside the neighborhood stays included, an endpoint of the neighborhood stays excluded.
Example 1 · co-g-negprop · level media · notes
Let $f(x) = (x - 1)^2 + 1$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -3\,f(x) + 4 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = -47$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = -51$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = 47$
Explanation. The constant $4$ is added outside and, with $a = -3 < 0$, $\min\{a f\} = a \max\{f\}$: the maximum of $f$ over $[0, 5]$ is $17$, at the endpoint $x = 5$ farthest from $1$, hence $z(P) = -3\cdot 17 + 4 = -47$. Typical mistake: using the minimum of $f$.
Example 2 · co-g-negprop · level media · notes
Let $f(x) = x^2 + 2$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -2\,f(x) + 5 \ \text{subject to}\ -2 \le x \le 1 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-2)$, $z(P) = -7$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-2)$, $z(P) = -12$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-2)$, $z(P) = 7$
Explanation. The constant $5$ is added outside and, with $a = -2 < 0$, $\min\{a f\} = a \max\{f\}$: the maximum of $f$ over $[-2, 1]$ is $6$, at the endpoint $x = -2$ farthest from $0$, hence $z(P) = -2\cdot 6 + 5 = -7$. Typical mistake: using the minimum of $f$.
Example 1 · co-g-localcubic · level media · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^3 - 3x + 4 \ \text{subject to}\ -4 \le x \le 3 \,\}$, with objective function $f(x) = x^3 - 3\,x + 4$ and zero derivative at $x = -1$ and $x = 1$. What are the local minimum points of $f$ in $F = [-4, 3]$ and the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$?
  1. local minimum points: $-4,\ 1$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-4)$
  2. local minimum points: $-4,\ 1$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$
  3. local minimum points: $1$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$
  4. local minimum points: $-1,\ 3$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$
Explanation. $f$ increases on $[-4, -1]$, decreases on $[-1, 1]$ and increases on $[1, 3]$: the local minimum points are $x = 1$ and also the endpoint $x = -4$ (near it, in $F$, $f$ does not go below $f(-4)$). Comparing $f(-4) = -48$ and $f(1) = 2$, the optimal solution is $x = -4$. Typical mistake: forgetting the endpoints of the feasible region.
Example 2 · co-g-localcubic · level media · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^3 - 6x^2 + 16 \ \text{subject to}\ -3 \le x \le 5 \,\}$, with objective function $f(x) = (x - 2)^3 - 12\,(x - 2) $ and zero derivative at $x = 0$ and $x = 4$. What are the local minimum points of $f$ in $F = [-3, 5]$ and the optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$?
  1. local minimum points: $-3,\ 4$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-3)$
  2. local minimum points: $-3,\ 4$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (4)$
  3. local minimum points: $4$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (4)$
  4. local minimum points: $0,\ 5$; optimal solution $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$
Explanation. $f$ increases on $[-3, 0]$, decreases on $[0, 4]$ and increases on $[4, 5]$: the local minimum points are $x = 4$ and also the endpoint $x = -3$ (near it, in $F$, $f$ does not go below $f(-3)$). Comparing $f(-3) = -65$ and $f(4) = -16$, the optimal solution is $x = -3$. Typical mistake: forgetting the endpoints of the feasible region.
Example 1 · co-g-multiopt · level media · notes
For the objective function $f(x) = \max\{\, 0,\; |x| - 2 \,\}$ over the feasible region $F = [-4, 5]$, what are the sets of optimal solutions $\arg\min\{ f(x) \text{ subject to } x \in F \}$ and $\arg\max\{ f(x) \text{ subject to } x \in F \}$?
  1. $\arg\min = [-2, 2]$, $\arg\max = \{5\}$
  2. $\arg\min = \{0\}$, $\arg\max = \{5\}$
  3. $\arg\min = [-2, 2]$, $\arg\max = \{-4\}$
  4. $\arg\min = [-2, 2]$, $\arg\max = \{-4, 5\}$
Explanation. $f(x) = 0$ for $-2 \le x \le 2$ and $f(x) = |x| - 2$ elsewhere: in $F$ the minimum value $0$ is attained on all of $[-2, 2]$ (infinitely many optimal solutions). The maximum is at the endpoints: $f(-4) = 2$, $f(5) = 3$.
Example 2 · co-g-multiopt · level media · notes
For the objective function $f(x) = \max\{\, 0,\; |x + 1| - 2 \,\}$ over the feasible region $F = [-3, 2]$, what are the sets of optimal solutions $\arg\min\{ f(x) \text{ subject to } x \in F \}$ and $\arg\max\{ f(x) \text{ subject to } x \in F \}$?
  1. $\arg\min = [-3, 1]$, $\arg\max = \{2\}$
  2. $\arg\min = \{-1\}$, $\arg\max = \{2\}$
  3. $\arg\min = [-3, 1]$, $\arg\max = \{-3\}$
  4. $\arg\min = [-3, 1]$, $\arg\max = \{-3, 2\}$
Explanation. $f(x) = 0$ for $-3 \le x \le 1$ and $f(x) = |x + 1| - 2$ elsewhere: in $F$ the minimum value $0$ is attained on all of $[-3, 1]$ (infinitely many optimal solutions). The maximum is at the endpoints: $f(-3) = 0$, $f(2) = 1$.
Example 1 · co-g-localglobal · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, f(x) \ \text{subject to}\ -2 \le x \le 4 \,\}$ with $f(x) = \min\{\, (x + 1)^2 + 3,\; (x - 3)^2 \,\}$ and optimal value $z(P)$. Which point is a local minimum point of $f$ in $[-2, 4]$ that is <em>not</em> an optimal solution, and what is $z(P)$?
  1. $x = -1$; optimal value $z(P) = 0$
  2. $x = 3$; optimal value $z(P) = 0$
  3. $x = -1$; optimal value $z(P) = 3$
  4. $x = 3$; optimal value $z(P) = 3$
Explanation. Near $x = -1$ we have $f(x) = (x + 1)^2 + 3 \ge 3$ and near $x = 3$ we have $f(x) = (x - 3)^2 \ge 0$: both are local minimum points, with local minima $3$ and $0$. Since $f(x) \ge 0$ everywhere, the optimal solution is $x = 3$ with $z(P) = 0$, and $x = -1$ is a local minimum that is not global (the endpoints $-2$ and $4$ are local maximum points).
Example 2 · co-g-localglobal · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, f(x) \ \text{subject to}\ -4 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ with $f(x) = \min\{\, (x + 3)^2 + 1,\; (x - 3)^2 \,\}$ and optimal value $z(P)$. Which point is a local minimum point of $f$ in $[-4, 5]$ that is <em>not</em> an optimal solution, and what is $z(P)$?
  1. $x = -3$; optimal value $z(P) = 0$
  2. $x = 3$; optimal value $z(P) = 0$
  3. $x = -3$; optimal value $z(P) = 1$
  4. $x = 3$; optimal value $z(P) = 1$
Explanation. Near $x = -3$ we have $f(x) = (x + 3)^2 + 1 \ge 1$ and near $x = 3$ we have $f(x) = (x - 3)^2 \ge 0$: both are local minimum points, with local minima $1$ and $0$. Since $f(x) \ge 0$ everywhere, the optimal solution is $x = 3$ with $z(P) = 0$, and $x = -3$ is a local minimum that is not global (the endpoints $-4$ and $5$ are local maximum points).
Example 1 · co-g-gap · level complessa · notes
For the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -2\,(x + 1)^2 - 1 \ \text{subject to}\ -4 \le x \le 0 \,\}$, with optimal value $z(P)$, the feasible solutions $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} = (-2)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)} = (-1)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(3)} = (-3)$ are known. What is the best bound on $z(P)$ that they provide, and what is $z(P)$?
  1. best bound $z(P) \le -9$; optimal value $z(P) = -19$
  2. best bound $z(P) \le -1$; optimal value $z(P) = -19$
  3. best bound $z(P) \le -9$; optimal value $z(P) = -1$
  4. best bound $-9 \le z(P)$; optimal value $z(P) = -19$
Explanation. The objective values are $-3$, $-1$, $-9$; in minimization form they give upper bounds and the best one is the smallest, $-9$. The parabola has vertex at $x = -1$ and opens downward: the optimum is at $x = -4$, with $z(P) = -19$.
Example 2 · co-g-gap · level complessa · notes
For the problem $P$: $\max\{\, -2\,(x - 3)^2 + 2 \ \text{subject to}\ -3 \le x \le 2 \,\}$, with optimal value $z(P)$, the feasible solutions $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(1)} = (0)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(2)} = (-3)$, $\bar{\boldsymbol x}^{(3)} = (-2)$ are known. What is the best bound on $z(P)$ that they provide, and what is $z(P)$?
  1. best bound $-16 \le z(P)$; optimal value $z(P) = 0$
  2. best bound $-70 \le z(P)$; optimal value $z(P) = 0$
  3. best bound $-16 \le z(P)$; optimal value $z(P) = 2$
  4. best bound $z(P) \le -16$; optimal value $z(P) = 0$
Explanation. The objective values are $-16$, $-70$, $-48$; in maximization form they give lower bounds and the best one is the largest, $-16$. The parabola has vertex at $x = 3$ and opens downward: the optimum is at $x = 2$, with $z(P) = 0$.

6. Convex and concave optimization problems (13)

Example 1 · co-g-boxproj · level media · notes
The problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1 - 6)^2 + (x_2 - 3)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ 0 \le x_1 \le 4,~ 0 \le x_2 \le 4 \,\}$ looks for the point of the rectangle closest to $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 36$
Explanation. The objective function is the sum of two terms in different variables, and each is smallest when the coordinate of $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ is brought back into its interval: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$. The point $\begin{pmatrix} 6 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ is not feasible, and the closest vertex is not optimal.
Example 2 · co-g-boxproj · level media · notes
The problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1 - 3)^2 + (x_2 - 3)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x_1 \le 1,~ 2 \le x_2 \le 4 \,\}$ looks for the point of the rectangle closest to $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 16$
Explanation. The objective function is the sum of two terms in different variables, and each is smallest when the coordinate of $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ is brought back into its interval: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$. The point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ is not feasible, and the closest vertex is not optimal.
Example 1 · co-g-1dproblem · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\max\{\, -x^2 + 2x + 5 \ \text{subject to}\ -2 \le x \le 2 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value). Which statement is correct?
  1. It is a concave maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 6$
  2. It is a convex maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 6$
  3. It is a concave maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = 5$
  4. It is a concave maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-2)$, $z(P) = -3$
Explanation. $f(x) = -(x - 1)^2 + 6$ is concave and $F = [-2, 2]$ is convex: it is a concave maximization problem. The derivative $-2x + 2$ vanishes at $x = 1$, which is feasible; $x = 1$ is a point of local maximum and hence optimal, because in a concave maximization problem every local maximum is optimal: $z(P) = 6$.
Example 2 · co-g-1dproblem · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, 3x^2 - 18x + 26 \ \text{subject to}\ 2 \le x \le 5 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value). Which statement is correct?
  1. It is a convex minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = -1$
  2. It is a concave minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = -1$
  3. It is a convex minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (6)$, $z(P) = 26$
  4. It is a convex minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (5)$, $z(P) = 11$
Explanation. $f(x) = 3\,(x - 3)^2 - 1$ is convex and $F = [2, 5]$ is convex: it is a convex minimization problem. The derivative $6x - 18$ vanishes at $x = 3$, which is feasible; $x = 3$ is a point of local minimum and hence optimal, because in a convex minimization problem every local minimum is optimal: $z(P) = -1$.
Example 1 · co-g-cubic · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x^3 - 3x^2 - 9x + 15 \ \text{subject to}\ -3 \le x \le 1 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value), whose objective function can also be written as $(x - 1)^3 - 12\,(x - 1) + 4$. Which statement is correct?
  1. It is a concave maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 20$
  2. It is a convex maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 20$
  3. It is a concave maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = -12$
  4. It is a concave maximization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 4$
Explanation. With $a = p - 1$ and $b = w - 1$ nonpositive, the inequality of cubes $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \ge \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ (the rest is linear) shows that $f$ is concave over $[-3, 1]$: it is a concave maximization problem. The derivative $3(x - 1)^2 - 12$ vanishes at $-1$ and $3$; only $-1$ is feasible, it is a local optimum and hence optimal (in problems of this kind local optima are global), with $z(P) = 20$.
Example 2 · co-g-cubic · level complessa · notes
Consider the problem $P$: $\min\{\, x^3 + 3x^2 - 9x - 12 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 3 \,\}$ (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value), whose objective function can also be written as $(x + 1)^3 - 12\,(x + 1) - 1$. Which statement is correct?
  1. It is a convex minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = -17$
  2. It is a concave minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = -17$
  3. It is a convex minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-3)$, $z(P) = 15$
  4. It is a convex minimization problem; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = -1$
Explanation. With $a = p + 1$ and $b = w + 1$ nonnegative, the inequality of cubes $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ (the rest is linear) shows that $f$ is convex over $[-1, 3]$: it is a convex minimization problem. The derivative $3(x + 1)^2 - 12$ vanishes at $-3$ and $1$; only $1$ is feasible, it is a local optimum and hence optimal (in problems of this kind local optima are global), with $z(P) = -17$.
Example 1 · co-g-sepbox · level complessa · notes
Consider the convex minimization problem $P$: $\min\{\, 2x_1^2 + 2x_2^2 - 12x_1 - 12x_2 \ \text{subject to}\ -2 \le x_1 \le 0,~ 2 \le x_2 \le 4 \,\}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -18$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -36$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 16$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -16$
Explanation. Completing the squares, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2(x_1 - 3)^2 + 2(x_2 - 3)^2 - 36$. The point $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ is not feasible; each term depends on a single variable and is smallest when $3$ is brought back into $[-2, 0]$ and $3$ into $[2, 4]$: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -18$.
Example 2 · co-g-sepbox · level complessa · notes
Consider the convex minimization problem $P$: $\min\{\, 2x_1^2 + 3x_2^2 - 8x_1 - 18x_2 \ \text{subject to}\ 1 \le x_1 \le 4,~ -2 \le x_2 \le 1 \,\}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -23$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -35$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 42$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -21$
Explanation. Completing the squares, $f({\boldsymbol x}) = 2(x_1 - 2)^2 + 3(x_2 - 3)^2 - 35$. The point $\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ is not feasible; each term depends on a single variable and is smallest when $2$ is brought back into $[1, 4]$ and $3$ into $[-2, 1]$: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -23$.
Example 1 · co-g-convtype · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. What can be said of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, f(x) = x^3 + 3 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in F \,\}$, with $F = \{\, x \in \R : x^2 \ge 4 \,\}$?
  1. it is neither convex nor concave, because $F$ is not convex
  2. it is a convex problem: $F$ is convex and $f$ is convex on $F$
  3. it is a concave problem: $F$ is convex and $f$ is concave on $F$
  4. it is neither convex nor concave, because $f$ is neither on $F$
Explanation. $F = \{\, x \in \R : x^2 \ge 4 \,\}$ is not convex (it contains two points but not the segment between them); $x^3$ is convex where $x \ge 0$ and concave where $x \le 0$ (inequality of cubes). The form (minimization or maximization) does not matter in the definition.
Example 2 · co-g-convtype · level facile · notes
An optimization problem with a convex feasible region is convex if the objective function is convex on the region, concave if it is concave. What can be said of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, f(x) = 2(x + 1)^2 + 3 \ \text{subject to}\ x \in F \,\}$, with $F = [0, 4]$?
  1. it is a convex problem: $F$ is convex and $f$ is convex on $F$
  2. it is a concave problem: $F$ is convex and $f$ is concave on $F$
  3. it is neither convex nor concave, because $F$ is not convex
  4. it is neither convex nor concave, because $f$ is neither on $F$
Explanation. $F = [0, 4]$ is an interval, hence convex; $2(x + 1)^2 + 3$ is convex (coefficient of $x^2$ positive). The form (minimization or maximization) does not matter in the definition.
Example 1 · co-g-cubeineq · level facile · notes
For every $\lambda \in [0,1]$ and $a, b \in \R$ we have $\lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3 - \big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 = \lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2\big((1+\lambda)a + (2-\lambda)b\big)$. What is this difference for $a = -2$, $b = -3$ and $\lambda = \frac{3}{4}$?
  1. $-\frac{87}{64}$
  2. $\frac{87}{64}$
  3. $-\frac{93}{64}$
  4. $-\frac{29}{4}$
Explanation. $\lambda(1-\lambda) = \frac{3}{16}$, $(a-b)^2 = 1$ and $(1+\lambda)a + (2-\lambda)b = -\frac{29}{4}$: the product is $-\frac{87}{64}$. With $a, b \le 0$ the difference is nonpositive, as the observation on cubes states.
Example 2 · co-g-cubeineq · level facile · notes
For every $\lambda \in [0,1]$ and $a, b \in \R$ we have $\lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3 - \big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 = \lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^2\big((1+\lambda)a + (2-\lambda)b\big)$. What is this difference for $a = 3$, $b = 1$ and $\lambda = \frac{1}{3}$?
  1. $\frac{136}{27}$
  2. $-\frac{136}{27}$
  3. $\frac{152}{27}$
  4. $\frac{68}{3}$
Explanation. $\lambda(1-\lambda) = \frac{2}{9}$, $(a-b)^2 = 4$ and $(1+\lambda)a + (2-\lambda)b = \frac{17}{3}$: the product is $\frac{136}{27}$. With $a, b \ge 0$ the difference is nonnegative, as the observation on cubes states.
Example 1 · co-g-concmin · level facile · notes
In the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -2 - (x - 2)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -1 \le x \le 3 \,\}$ the objective function is concave: it is not a convex minimization problem. What are the local minimum points, the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. local minima at $x = -1$ and $x = 3$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = -11$
  2. local minima at $x = 2$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (2)$, $z(P) = -2$
  3. local minima at $x = -1$ and $x = 3$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (3)$, $z(P) = -3$
  4. local minima at $x = -1$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = -11$
Explanation. The function increases up to $x = 2$ (maximum point, value $-2$) and then decreases: both endpoints are local minimum points, with values $f(-1) = -11$ and $f(3) = -3$. The optimal solution is the endpoint farthest from $2$, $x = -1$; the other is a local minimum that is not global, which is impossible in a convex minimization problem.
Example 2 · co-g-concmin · level facile · notes
In the problem $P$: $\min\{\, 8 - (x + 1)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -4 \le x \le 1 \,\}$ the objective function is concave: it is not a convex minimization problem. What are the local minimum points, the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. local minima at $x = -4$ and $x = 1$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-4)$, $z(P) = -1$
  2. local minima at $x = -1$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 8$
  3. local minima at $x = -4$ and $x = 1$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 4$
  4. local minima at $x = -4$; $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-4)$, $z(P) = -1$
Explanation. The function increases up to $x = -1$ (maximum point, value $8$) and then decreases: both endpoints are local minimum points, with values $f(-4) = -1$ and $f(1) = 4$. The optimal solution is the endpoint farthest from $-1$, $x = -4$; the other is a local minimum that is not global, which is impossible in a convex minimization problem.
Example 1 · co-g-lpbox · level facile · notes
The linear objective function of the problem $P$: $\max\{\, x_1 + x_2 \ \text{subject to}\ 1 \le x_1 \le 4,~ -2 \le x_2 \le 0 \,\}$ is both convex and concave, and the rectangle is convex. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 2$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -1$
Explanation. Each term depends on a single variable: in maximization form, $x_i$ goes to its lower bound if its coefficient is negative, to its upper bound otherwise. Hence $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $z(P) = 4$. Typical mistake: getting the direction wrong for a negative coefficient.
Example 2 · co-g-lpbox · level facile · notes
The linear objective function of the problem $P$: $\min\{\, -x_1 + 3x_2 \ \text{subject to}\ -2 \le x_1 \le 1,~ -1 \le x_2 \le 2 \,\}$ is both convex and concave, and the rectangle is convex. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 8$
Explanation. Each term depends on a single variable: in minimization form, $x_i$ goes to its lower bound if its coefficient is positive, to its upper bound otherwise. Hence $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $z(P) = -4$. Typical mistake: getting the direction wrong for a negative coefficient.
Example 1 · co-g-cubicregion · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = (x - 1)^3 - 12\,(x - 1) + 4$. For which feasible region $F$ is the problem $\max\{\, f(x) \ \text{subject to}\ x \in F \,\}$ a concave maximization problem?
  1. $F = [-3, 1]$
  2. $F = [1, 5]$
  3. $F = [0, 4]$
  4. $F = [2, 6]$
Explanation. With $a = p - 1$ and $b = w - 1$, the inequality of cubes gives $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ if $a, b \ge 0$ and $\ge$ if $a, b \le 0$; the term $-12\,(x - 1)$ is linear. Hence $f$ is convex on intervals with $x \ge 1$ and concave on intervals with $x \le 1$: we need $F = [-3, 1]$. An interval containing $1$ in its interior does not work.
Example 2 · co-g-cubicregion · level facile · notes
Let $f(x) = x^3 - 12\,x - 1$. For which feasible region $F$ is the problem $\max\{\, f(x) \ \text{subject to}\ x \in F \,\}$ a concave maximization problem?
  1. $F = [-2, 0]$
  2. $F = [0, 2]$
  3. $F = [-1, 1]$
  4. $F = [1, 3]$
Explanation. With $a = p - 0$ and $b = w - 0$, the inequality of cubes gives $\big(\lambda a + (1-\lambda) b\big)^3 \le \lambda a^3 + (1-\lambda) b^3$ if $a, b \ge 0$ and $\ge$ if $a, b \le 0$; the term $-12\,x$ is linear. Hence $f$ is convex on intervals with $x \ge 0$ and concave on intervals with $x \le 0$: we need $F = [-2, 0]$. An interval containing $0$ in its interior does not work.
Example 1 · co-g-diskproj · level media · notes
The convex problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1 + 12)^2 + (x_2 + 9)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1^2 + x_2^2 \le 100 \,\}$ looks for the point of the disk with center $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $10$ closest to $\begin{pmatrix} -12 \\ -9 \end{pmatrix}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ -6 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 25$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ -6 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -12 \\ -9 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -10 \\ -9 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
Explanation. The point $\begin{pmatrix} -12 \\ -9 \end{pmatrix}$ has norm $15 > 10$, so it is not feasible; the closest point of the disk lies on the segment towards the center, at distance $10$ from the origin: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \frac{10}{15}\begin{pmatrix} -12 \\ -9 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} -8 \\ -6 \end{pmatrix}$. The objective function is the <em>squared</em> distance: $z(P) = 5^2 = 25$. Typical mistake: bringing each coordinate back into $[-10, 10]$, as for a square.
Example 2 · co-g-diskproj · level media · notes
The convex problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1 + 6)^2 + (x_2 - 8)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1^2 + x_2^2 \le 25 \,\}$ looks for the point of the disk with center $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and radius $5$ closest to $\begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ 8 \end{pmatrix}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 25$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 5$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ 8 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -5 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 10$
Explanation. The point $\begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ 8 \end{pmatrix}$ has norm $10 > 5$, so it is not feasible; the closest point of the disk lies on the segment towards the center, at distance $5$ from the origin: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \frac{5}{10}\begin{pmatrix} -6 \\ 8 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$. The objective function is the <em>squared</em> distance: $z(P) = 5^2 = 25$. Typical mistake: bringing each coordinate back into $[-5, 5]$, as for a square.
Example 1 · co-g-halfplane · level media · notes
The convex problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1 - 3)^2 + (x_2 - 3)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 \le 4,~ x_1 \ge 0,~ x_2 \ge 0 \,\}$ looks for the feasible point closest to $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 2$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 1$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
Explanation. $\begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}$ violates $x_1 + x_2 \le 4$ ($6 > 4$). The closest point of the line $x_1 + x_2 = 4$ is obtained by subtracting the same amount $\frac{6 - 4}{2} = 1$ from both coordinates: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, which is feasible, and $z(P) = 1^2 + 1^2 = 2$. Moving a single coordinate gives a feasible but farther point.
Example 2 · co-g-halfplane · level media · notes
The convex problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x_1 - 4)^2 + (x_2 - 4)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 \le 4,~ x_1 \ge 0,~ x_2 \ge 0 \,\}$ looks for the feasible point closest to $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 8$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 0$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 16$
Explanation. $\begin{pmatrix} 4 \\ 4 \end{pmatrix}$ violates $x_1 + x_2 \le 4$ ($8 > 4$). The closest point of the line $x_1 + x_2 = 4$ is obtained by subtracting the same amount $\frac{8 - 4}{2} = 2$ from both coordinates: $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}$, which is feasible, and $z(P) = 2^2 + 2^2 = 8$. Moving a single coordinate gives a feasible but farther point.
Example 1 · co-g-meanpoint · level media · notes
The problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x + 2)^2 + (x + 1)^2 + (x - 4)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -3 \le x \le -1 \,\}$ is a convex minimization problem (sum of convex functions over an interval). What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 26$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (\frac{1}{3})$, $z(P) = \frac{62}{3}$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-3)$, $z(P) = 54$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-2)$, $z(P) = 37$
Explanation. The derivative $2\big(3x - (-2) - (-1) - (4)\big)$ vanishes at the mean $x = \frac{1}{3}$, which is not feasible: the function is convex and decreases up to the mean, so the optimum is the closest endpoint, $x = -1$; $z(P) = 26$. Typical mistake: using the median $-1$, which minimizes the sum of absolute values, not of squares.
Example 2 · co-g-meanpoint · level media · notes
The problem $P$: $\min\{\, (x + 1)^2 + x^2 + (x - 4)^2 \ \text{subject to}\ -3 \le x \le 0 \,\}$ is a convex minimization problem (sum of convex functions over an interval). What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (0)$, $z(P) = 17$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (1)$, $z(P) = 14$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-3)$, $z(P) = 62$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = (-1)$, $z(P) = 26$
Explanation. The derivative $2\big(3x - (-1) - (0) - (4)\big)$ vanishes at the mean $x = 1$, which is not feasible: the function is convex and decreases up to the mean, so the optimum is the closest endpoint, $x = 0$; $z(P) = 17$. Typical mistake: using the median $0$, which minimizes the sum of absolute values, not of squares.
Example 1 · co-g-eqconstr · level complessa · notes
The problem $P$: $\min\{\, 3x_1^2 + 2x_2^2 + 2x_1 + 5x_2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 = -2 \,\}$ is convex (convex objective function, linear constraint). Substituting $x_2 = -2 - x_1$ it becomes a problem in one variable. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -\frac{1}{2} \\ -\frac{3}{2} \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -\frac{13}{4}$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -\frac{1}{3} \\ -\frac{5}{4} \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -\frac{83}{24}$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{11}{2} \\ -\frac{15}{2} \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = \frac{707}{4}$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -2$
Explanation. With $g(x_1) = f(x_1, -2 - x_1)$, $g'(x_1) = 6x_1 - 4(-2 - x_1) - 3 = 0$ gives $x_1 = -\frac{1}{2}$, hence $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} -\frac{1}{2} \\ -\frac{3}{2} \end{pmatrix}$ and $z(P) = -\frac{13}{4}$; $g$ is convex, and the local minimum is the optimal solution. The point $\begin{pmatrix} -\frac{1}{3} \\ -\frac{5}{4} \end{pmatrix}$, where the gradient of $f$ vanishes, does not satisfy the constraint.
Example 2 · co-g-eqconstr · level complessa · notes
The problem $P$: $\min\{\, 2x_1^2 + x_2^2 - 5x_1 - 3x_2 \ \text{subject to}\ x_1 + x_2 = -1 \,\}$ is convex (convex objective function, linear constraint). Substituting $x_2 = -1 - x_1$ it becomes a problem in one variable. What are the optimal solution and the optimal value (with $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P)$ the optimal solution and $z(P)$ the optimal value)?
  1. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 4$
  2. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{5}{4} \\ \frac{3}{2} \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = -\frac{43}{8}$
  3. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ -3 \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = 16$
  4. $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{2} \\ -\frac{3}{2} \end{pmatrix}$, $z(P) = \frac{19}{4}$
Explanation. With $g(x_1) = f(x_1, -1 - x_1)$, $g'(x_1) = 4x_1 - 2(-1 - x_1) - 2 = 0$ gives $x_1 = 0$, hence $\tilde{\boldsymbol x}(P) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ -1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $z(P) = 4$; $g$ is convex, and the local minimum is the optimal solution. The point $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{5}{4} \\ \frac{3}{2} \end{pmatrix}$, where the gradient of $f$ vanishes, does not satisfy the constraint.
